539. The Valence of Compassion
Deonna, J. (2026). The Valence of Compassion. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10501-7
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper addresses a central phenomenological puzzle: how to reconcile the traditional view of compassion as an instance of suffering with contemporary neuroscientific findings characterizing it as being hedonically positive. Building on the attitudinalist framework, I argue that this conflict arises from a failure to see emotions as unfolding episodes. By thinking of emotions as felt readinesses to act, we can understand compassion’s valence not as a fixed hedonic tone, but as a dynamic trajectory within a structured affective space. The argument proceeds in three stages. First, I establish that positive and negative emotions are defined by their structural orientation, welcoming or rejecting value. Second, I apply this to compassion, arguing it is fundamentally a mode of taking displeasure in another’s suffering. Finally, I show that the “positivity” noted by scientists is the result of the benevolent action tendency being allowed to run its course, transforming initial distress into the expansive experience of relief and connection.

544. Presumption of guilt? AI chatbots and the role of affirmation vivas in assessing academic integrity
Armstrong, L. & Karyotis, G. (2026). Presumption of guilt? AI chatbots and the role of affirmation vivas in assessing academic integrity. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01505-5
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial Intelligence (AI) and the proliferation of conversational agents have brought transformative changes in education but pose challenges in ensuring the academic integrity of assessments. This article proposes ‘affirmation vivas’ as a potent response to reasonable doubts about the authorship of assessed student work. It draws on a case-study of the first affirmation viva conducted at the University of Glasgow, conducted after ChatGPT “confessed” to generating part of a student dissertation. During the viva, while acknowledging experimenting with AI chatbots, the student successfully demonstrated understanding and ownership of their work. 548. Disability rights in the Commonwealth Caribbean: a capability and intersectional critique

https://doi.org/10.1017/s1744552326100706
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper offers the largest empirical study to date of the rights of persons with disabilities in the Commonwealth Caribbean. 549. Legal fictions in birth registration: linguistic manifestations and social implications of paternity attribution in Ukraine and comparative perspectives

Abstract (original): Abstract This article offers a comprehensive legal, linguistic, sociodemographic and psychological analysis of the phenomenon of mandatory fictitious paternity in civil status registration in Ukraine, as provided for in Part 1 of Article 135 of the Family Code of Ukraine. 553. Gestational Harm and Responsibility: A Vulnerability Framework Approach
McMahon, C. (2026). Gestational Harm and Responsibility: A Vulnerability Framework Approach. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70212
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Gestational harm occurs when exposures in‐utero, such as alcohol, lead to long‐lasting health effects in the child born of that pregnancy. Because the actions of pregnant people can bring about such harm, this has given rise to debate about whether pregnant people are morally obligated to prevent harm to their future children (call this ‘gestational responsibility’). The debate on gestational responsibility increasingly recognises the social context of gestational harm. To the extent that unequal structural conditions constrain pregnant people's ability to prevent harms to their future children, some scholars argue that collective agents, and not individual pregnant people, are primarily responsible for such harms. In this, the debate has neglected to seriously consider the question of individual gestational responsibility under conditions of structural inequality. To address this question, I develop a framework for gestational responsibility grounded in the concept of vulnerability. The concept of vulnerability has gained traction in feminist bioethics as a means of capturing relational autonomy and increased susceptibility to harm under unequal social conditions. Focusing on the context of foetal alcohol spectrum disorder, I argue that the metaphor of ‘layers’ of vulnerability can help to articulate the varying degrees of autonomy—and correspondingly responsibility—of pregnant people under conditions of structural inequality. The purpose of my argument is to demonstrate the differential degrees of responsibility that people (in this case, those who are pregnant) possess in contexts of inequality, and to show how inequality mitigates responsibility.

559. New Materialism, Harm, and Hierarchies in Nature
Piccolo, S. (2026). New Materialism, Harm, and Hierarchies in Nature. Political Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00905917261499990
Abstract (original): This article is about an internal contradiction within the ascendant philosophy of new materialism, namely that its ethical opposition to harm clashes with its ontology. In the first part, I summarize new materialists’ attention to material entanglements and rejection of hierarchies in nature. In the second section, I argue that diagnosing and addressing harm to ecosystems and individuals (almost) always depend upon a hierarchy of beings within that ecosystem and a set of pre-determined potentials/preferences of how individual entities should be, two principles that new materialists explicitly reject. 563. Knowing the better, believing the worse: a commitment account of epistemic supererogation
Jackson, E.G. (2026). Knowing the better, believing the worse: a commitment account of epistemic supererogation. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02665-0
Abstract (original): Abstract Epistemic supererogation involves going beyond what epistemic rationality requires. If some beliefs can be epistemically supererogatory, then others are epistemically suboptimal yet still rational. In ethics, knowingly doing the suboptimal is commonplace—we often spend money on ourselves rather than giving it to charity. By contrast, existing accounts of epistemic supererogation deny that one could knowingly adopt an epistemically suboptimal attitude. I challenge this assumption and develop a commitment-based account of epistemic supererogation. I focus on cases involving religious, moral, practical, or interpersonal commitments, where the evidence allows more than one rational attitude. In these cases, a longstanding commitment can help explain why someone reasonably sticks with a belief that is epistemically suboptimal. I then consider two ways of explaining this: one on which the belief remains rational because it fits the evidence well enough, and another on which practical factors affect epistemic rationality. This account strengthens the analogy between ethics and epistemology by showing that, in some cases, we can knowingly—and rationally—decline to take the epistemically best option.

564. Imperatives and Deontic Modality: An Inferential Expressivist Perspective

Abstract (original): Abstract This paper is divided into two main parts. 567. What Is Time?
Baron, S. & Eskens, F. (2026). What Is Time?. Noûs.
https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70078
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT We argue that time is the great enabler: it is that which makes causation and change possible. We develop this idea into a functionalist account of time that has some very nice features: (i) it differentiates time from space where needed, (ii) it illuminates time via its connection to causation and change, (iii) it accounts for the apparent multiple realizability of time, and (iv) it correctly classifies worlds that have time as such. We compare our account to three rivals: time as the great necessitator, time as the great informer, and time as the measure of change. We show that treating time as an enabler does better.

568. Metasemantics and the Continuum Hypothesis
Barton, N.A. (2026). Metasemantics and the Continuum Hypothesis. Noûs.
https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70079
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The Continuum Hypothesis featured top of Hilbert's list of 23 problems in 1900. Today, we still consider the question, with various programmes pulling in different directions. This conceptual diversity raises a puzzle: In what sense do we disagree when we talk about it? A standard assumption takes the content of our thought about classes and the Continuum Hypothesis to be uniform between agents . I argue that moderate views of content determination lead to a rejection of this assumption. However, I also argue that whilst the Continuum Hypothesis can have different content for different agents, it can also be determinate for them. In particular, I suggest that there is a fault line between those who think there are uncountable sets and recent countabilist views.

569. Institutionalized Distrust and Monitoring in Means‐Tested Benefits Programs
Lajoie, C. (2026). Institutionalized Distrust and Monitoring in Means‐Tested Benefits Programs. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70147
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The dominant view in philosophy of trust is that trust and monitoring are opposed. I argue that not all forms of monitoring necessarily undermine trust, but that certain forms of it do because they signal distrust of those being monitored. Crucially, this type of signaling is not confined to interpersonal contexts. I defend the view that institutions can signal distrust and offer the concept of institutionalized distrust to describe a form of distrust that does not reside in individual attitudes, but is instead expressed through policies, practices, and procedures, including forms of monitoring that are intrusive or disproportionate. My primary example is the eligibility procedures of the Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program (SNAP) in the United States. I show that food assistance recipients encounter distrust in their interactions with the institutions they rely on for support and whose distrust may, in turn, influence attitudes of distrust among the public.

570. Local Housing Discretion and the Affordability Override
Quigley, T. (2026). Local Housing Discretion and the Affordability Override. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70148
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The crucial issue in housing policy is the appropriate degree of community discretion over housing development. I develop an improved account of the normative value of community discretion, building on the literature on gentrification. This shows that community discretion does have genuine value. But more community discretion tends to lead to higher housing prices, which are themselves a significant cause of gentrification and other harms. We need policies that appropriately reflect this tension. Land value taxation is one good approach; I suggest that a novel policy, the ‘affordability override’, is even better.

572. Islamic Values and Value–Behaviour Gaps in Saudi SMEs: A Qualitative Study of Leadership Practice
Alanazi, M. & Bourne, D.J. (2026). Islamic Values and Value–Behaviour Gaps in Saudi SMEs: A Qualitative Study of Leadership Practice. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06508-0
Abstract (original): Abstract The relationship between religiously grounded values and everyday leadership practice in organisations remains underexplored, particularly in small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs). In Saudi Arabia, Islamic values such as justice, trust, consultation, and accountability are expected to underpin ethical leadership and organisational conduct. Yet interview accounts from employees and leaders suggest systematic value–behaviour gaps in how these values are interpreted and enacted in practice. Drawing on 30 semi-structured interviews with one leader and five employees in each of five Saudi SMEs, this exploratory qualitative study examines how Islamic values are mobilised in leadership practice and how different patterns of value enactment shape employees’ experiences. Using an inductively derived three-level framework – balanced, excessive and insufficient enactment – the study illustrates how the same values can support ethical leadership when applied in a balanced way, but become associated with harmful or destructive leadership practices when applied in excess or deficiency. The findings show how Islamic values may be selectively internalised or performatively invoked, creating misalignments between professed values and everyday leadership behaviours. Drawing on moral identity theory and virtue ethics as complementary theoretical perspectives, the study explains two dimensions of value enactment. Moral identity theory illuminates how Islamic values are internalised, selectively enacted, or invoked performatively, while virtue ethics explains how values can become dysfunctional when enacted excessively, insufficiently or without proportion and contextual judgement. Together, these perspectives clarify how value–behaviour gaps emerge and shape employees’ experiences in Saudi SMEs.

573. A taste for what should not be wanted: generative AI and transgressive fantasy
Voinea, C. & Vica, C. (2026). A taste for what should not be wanted: generative AI and transgressive fantasy. Ethics Inf Technol.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10676-026-09929-7
Abstract (original): Abstract With generative AI, everyone can materialize whatever fantasy they can conjure​ up in their imagination, including transgressive ones. The ‘orthodox’ view on the moral status of fantasies, even of materialized ones, is that such content is outside the scope of morality, since fantasies are quarantined from the agent’s real-world beliefs and desires. In this paper, we challenge this view by arguing that even if we grant that that is the case, repeated engagement with fantasies can contribute to the cultivation of various affective dispositions, or tastes, that can themselves be morally criticizable. We then argue that generative AI allows users to enter a ‘co-creative feedback loop’, a near-instantaneous cycle of prompting, viewing, and refining one’s fantasy, which makes sustained engagement with the transgressive representations very easy. Our claim is that such repeated and focused engagement with transgressive content can contribute to the cultivation of a taste or sensibility for what is morally problematic in those fantasies. If such tastes are morally criticizable, then engaging in practices that cultivate them is also open to moral criticism, even when no one is directly harmed.

581. Algorithmic riba and algorithmic Shariah non-compliance: risk mechanisms and a proposed governance protocol for Islamic banks
Shareef, M. & Nizam, I. (2026). Algorithmic riba and algorithmic Shariah non-compliance: risk mechanisms and a proposed governance protocol for Islamic banks. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01497-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Machine learning (ML) systems are increasingly used in the credit, pricing, and collections decisions of financial institutions, including Islamic banks, yet Shariah oversight remains primarily contract-centred. This conceptual paper identifies three distinct pathways of algorithmic Shariah non-compliance. Algorithmic riba, used narrowly, concerns profit-rate models whose optimisation objective may reproduce the time-value logic prohibited by riba; conventional benchmarking of murabahah (cost-plus sale) margins is not itself impermissible. Algorithmic injustice concerns credit-scoring outcomes that may violate the ‘adl (justice) obligation, and algorithmic gharamah (penalty) concerns collections systems that may impose charges beyond ​ta’widh (compensation for documented loss). To support model-level review, we propose the Shariah Algorithmic Compliance Screen (SACS), a five-component protocol grounded in Maqasid al-Shariah and offered as a first-iteration design proposal. Model owners disclose use cases, training data, objective functions, and outputs; independent technical validators test the relevant model evidence; and Shariah Supervisory Boards (SSBs) determine compliance. Explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) methods are used descriptively, while established fairness diagnostics (subgroup calibration, error-rate comparison, and disparate-impact measurement) provide separate evidence on outcome equity. The three mechanisms are derived analytically from doctrine and the ML literature and are stated as conditional hypotheses. The study completes the construction stage of the design: SACS is conceptually grounded and operationally specified, and its components adapt existing artificial intelligence governance practice to jurisprudential review. SACS has not yet been evaluated. Practitioner evaluation, particularly in Small Island Developing States such as the Maldives, is the next stage and will assess intelligibility, implementation burden, and practical usefulness.

582. Who am I really talking to? Authenticity, transparency, and the case for “AI ticks” in AI-mediated communication
Battisti, D. et al. (2026). Who am I really talking to? Authenticity, transparency, and the case for “AI ticks” in AI-mediated communication. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01498-1
Abstract (original): Abstract The growing integration of artificial intelligence (AI) into everyday messaging systems is transforming the conditions under which interpersonal communication takes place. As AI-mediated communication (AI-MC) becomes increasingly seamless and context-sensitive, it blurs the boundary between human expression and algorithmic assistance. This development raises fundamental questions about potential challenges to authenticity in personal relationships and possible strategies to address them. In this regard, we make three main contributions. First, we offer a technically grounded analysis of emerging AI-MC architectures, distinguishing between external prompting, keyboard-level assistance, and fully integrated in-app co-authoring systems. Second, we develop a normative account of authenticity in AI-MC, understood as a second-person, relationship-sensitive notion, drawing on analytic theories of endorsement, relational normativity, and socially situated self-expression. On this basis, we argue that authenticity cannot be directly engineered by design, since it depends on reflective agency and context-dependent interpersonal obligations. Third, we advance a design-oriented proposal aimed at mitigating authenticity-related concerns without resorting to paternalistic restrictions: the introduction of optional and granular disclosure mechanisms (“AI ticks”) that allow users to signal the presence and degree of AI assistance in a context-sensitive manner. We argue that such forms of transparency function not as a substitute for authenticity, but as a pro-ethical condition that enables agents to negotiate authenticity-based obligations within their relationships. The analysis contributes to current debates on the ethics of AI-mediated communication and the conditions of responsible human–AI interaction.

591. Johan Willem Beyen and the creation of the internal market: Lessons from history and the social sciences for the micro-foundations of EU law

https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10090
Abstract (original): Abstract This article formalises and develops an individual-centred framework to explain what I call the micro-foundations of EU law, and applies it to Johan Willem Beyen’s role in the creation of the Common Market. It argues that standard structural accounts in European integration overlook the micro-foundations: the ways in which the desires, beliefs and choices of specific actors shape integration outcomes. Drawing on methodological individualism, mechanism-based causal explanation and narrative legal history, the article reconstructs how Beyen’s liberal economic convictions, his experience in international finance and Bretton Woods institutions, and his reading of the 1930s crisis and post-war power shifts informed his preference for a general common market over sectoral integration. 593. Voice of the Dead: Yorùbá Spiritual Justice, English Jurisprudence, and the Making of Inheritance Law in the British Colony of Lagos (1863–1938)

Abstract (original): Abstract In early colonial Lagos, inheritance disputes laid bare the exclusionary and discriminatory character of British jurisprudence. 598. Higher‐Order Tense Realism
Correia, F. & Rosenkranz, S. (2026). Higher‐Order Tense Realism. Noûs.
https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70073
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Realism about tense comes in various forms. In “Tense and Reality” (2005) and “The Reality of Tense” (2006), Kit Fine offered a helpful taxonomy that contributed to shape the current debate on the topic. In our paper “Eternal Facts in an Ageing Universe” (2012), we improved upon this taxonomy, identifying a further type of view that Fine leaves out: Dynamic Absolutism. Both these taxonomies construe the different versions of tense realism in terms of first‐order quantification over facts or states of affairs. Our goal is to show that the logical space of these first‐order tense‐realist positions can be replicated using higher‐order quantification instead. 599. The Choiceworthlessness of Transformative Heroism
Oidermaa, M.K. & Willners, M. (2026). The Choiceworthlessness of Transformative Heroism. PPA.
https://doi.org/10.1111/papa.70036
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT History abounds with transformative moral heroes—agents devoting their lives to uprooting entrenched unjust structures. Though philosophy has said much about heroes generally, this distinctive class remains undertheorized. This matters because they are among our most celebrated moral exemplars, and much of our sense of moral progress rests on them. Yet they characteristically act against familiar constraints on responsible deliberation, pursuing apparently infeasible and inefficacious projects while foreclosing tractable alternatives that would secure demonstrable goods. We establish this tension and argue that the most promising attempts to dissolve it fail. At best, this shows that moral theory is structurally conservative, as its standards of responsible deliberation systematically favor mitigation over transformation. At worst, it reveals a deeper instability in moral theory, as it seems to depend for its progress on agents it cannot vindicate.

600. Logical Quantification and the Hohfeldian Analysis of Rights
Kramer, M.H. (2026). Logical Quantification and the Hohfeldian Analysis of Rights. Ratio Juris.
https://doi.org/10.1111/raju.70035
Abstract (original): Abstract Ever since the American legal philosopher Wesley Hohfeld published his analyses of rights in the second decade of the twentieth century, numerous philosophers and legal theorists have grappled with his matrix of legal positions. Very seldom, however, have the commentators on Hohfeld recognized the importance of logical quantification for any endeavor to elaborate the categories of that matrix fully. This paper will mull over a few of the manifold ways in which quantification is operative in the legal relationships that Hohfeld delineated. After furnishing a short conspectus of the Hohfeldian analytical framework which distills those relationships, this paper will briefly discuss the two most prominent logical quantifiers and will then explore how one's awareness of the workings of those quantifiers can sharpen one's grasp of the Hohfeldian analysis.

601. A Response to Kramer on Hohfeld and Quantification
Halpin, A. (2026). A Response to Kramer on Hohfeld and Quantification. Ratio Juris.
https://doi.org/10.1111/raju.70036
Abstract (original): Abstract This response to Matthew Kramer's article on logical quantification and Hohfeldian analysis commences with reactions to some of his preliminary observations on the Hohfeldian scheme, and on his suggested forms of the universal quantifier: “aggregative,” “distributive,” and “disjunctive.” A detailed discussion then follows of Kramer's notion of a disjunctive universal quantifier, and how he sees this as a threat to Hohfeld's insistence on one‐to‐one correlation. 602. Russell, Moore, Idealism and Knowing What One Is Talking About
Hay, C. (2026). Russell, Moore, Idealism and Knowing What One Is Talking About. Theoria.
https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70134
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The topic addressed is persistent difficulties in writings on Russell and early Moore, of stating what it is one has in mind when thinking what there is. If propositions, whether before or after the multiple‐relations theory of judgement, are made up of the things themselves, then propositions cannot be the content of thought. In arguing for realism, Russell and Moore cannot open up a problem of the external world, as that would fail to refute idealism, but neither can they accept intermediary senses or ideas as these risk differing from what there is, precluding knowledge (cf. the Mont Blanc correspondence between Frege and Russell). Given Moore's and Russell's rejection of psychologism, what is left is an external relation of acquaintance as fully adequate representationism, as a condition on realism if we can be said to know what we are thinking and speaking about.

603. Hope for Our Children
Terlazzo, R. (2026). Hope for Our Children. J Moral Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1163/17455243-bja10221
Abstract (original): Abstract Helping our children to understand the unjust world around them can be a difficult task. Given the immensity of injustice, learning about the reality of the world might lead our children to anxiety, despair, or callous indifference. In this paper, I employ and develop the concept of hope to show how these harms can be avoided. In particular, I develop three different threats to children that knowledge of injustice might cause, and offer accounts of three kinds of hope that parents can help to foster in order to avoid those harms. These are hope for the world, hope for their children’s own futures, and hope for their children’s own moral characters. I end by considering whether other attitudes such as faith or righteous anger can serve the role that I have assigned to hope, and argue that they cannot.

605. Beyond status signalling: on the primacy of economy and ideology in class formation
Winter, D. (2026). Beyond status signalling: on the primacy of economy and ideology in class formation. Zeitschrift für Ethik und Moralphilosophie.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s42048-026-00263-7
Abstract (original): Abstract Hanno Sauer defines class as socially constructed scarcity generated by competition for status through hard-to-fake intangible signals and concludes that class inequality, although unjust, is practically ineradicable. This reply examines two premises of his account and then proposes an alternative foundation. First, economic resources do not stand on a par with cultural recognition, social contacts, power and prestige, since they constitute a necessary, though not sufficient, precondition of all of these factors. Second, the zero-sum thesis holds only to a limited extent, because intangible status resources can be scaled and divided and because quantitative changes alter the quality of a top position, so that social systems expand and generate new strata. Building on these two results, the reply argues that every ascription of class must be locatable within a matrix of economy and ideology, understood respectively as material resources and as values that cannot be captured in monetary terms. Each of these coordinates is necessary in itself and sufficient only in combination with the other, and all further distinguishing factors find their place within them. Situational changes of status, which Sauer regards as justified, accordingly occur only between positions that lie close together in this matrix. In all of this, the argument proceeds descriptively and does not assess class membership normatively.

606. Toward responsible convenience: a conceptual framework for promoting care in AI recommender systems

https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01487-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence (AI)-driven recommender systems increasingly mediate how we consume information, buy products, and form connections. In this conceptual paper, we argue that these systems operationalize two fundamental dimensions of convenience: immediacy and egocentrism. These dimensions of convenience orient users away from long-term, allocentric perspectives and the preservation of agency necessary for responsible engagement with digital technologies. To frame our analysis, we develop a bidimensional model of deliberative space shaped by two opposing forces, convenience and care, and introduce responsible convenience as a normative ideal and conceptual framework for ethical development, regulation, and use of AI-powered recommender systems. A close reading of Gary Shteyngart’s novel Super Sad True Love Story illustrates how narratives may prompt deliberation on the long-term consequences of algorithmically mediated human connection. Future empirical work could examine whether, how, and under what institutional conditions narrative engagement expands temporal and interpersonal horizons and translates care into behavioral, design, or regulatory change.

https://doi.org/10.1093/monist/onag030
Abstract (original): Abstract What makes biodiversity valuable? I offer a new account of the value of one aspect of biodiversity—diversity of sentient animal species. I propose and defend the Positive Engagement Principle (PEP): that it is intrinsically valuable that more aspects of reality are positively engaged with by sentient beings. I argue that diversity of sentient animals is instrumentally valuable because it enables a wider range of such engagements through the diverse forms of perception, understanding, appreciation, and action available to animals of different species. I compare PEP with two recent accounts of the value of diversity of animal experiences—Livermore’s Heteric Welfarism and James’s Lifeworld account—and argue that PEP better captures the distinctive value of world-directed experiences across species.

615. Activism and Disciplinary Ethical Progress
Rechnitzer, T. (2026). Activism and Disciplinary Ethical Progress. The Monist.
https://doi.org/10.1093/monist/onag035
Abstract (original): Abstract Academic ethicists who engage in activism frequently face two categories of objection: that they overstep their expertise by prescribing practical action, and that activism is incompatible with contributing to ethical progress. This paper focuses on the latter, investigating the extent to which activism is compatible with the discipline’s cognitive goals. I define activism as a partisan, sustained, and personal effort aimed at social change, and adopt a notion of disciplinary ethical progress based on the discipline’s ability to enable understanding in a maximally inclusive audience. On this basis, I articulate progress-based objections, distinguishing between “outward” threats to public uptake and “inward” threats to the integrity of inquiry. Drawing on feminist philosophy of science, I argue that activism can function as a corrective to “ivory tower” worries like isolation or triviality. I conclude by proposing an evaluative framework to assess the compatibility of specific activist engagements with disciplinary ethical progress.

616. Relational Equality and the Morality of Harming: Why Consequentialists and Nonconsequentialists Are Climbing Different Mountains
Kauppinen, A. (2026). Relational Equality and the Morality of Harming: Why Consequentialists and Nonconsequentialists Are Climbing Different Mountains. The Monist.
https://doi.org/10.1093/monist/onag036
Abstract (original): Abstract Derek Parfit famously argues that the best versions of (rule) consequentialism and nonconsequentialism converge—roughly, that impersonally optimific moral principles are uniquely universally willable and alternatives to them reasonably rejectable. An important part of making this case is arguing that the key nonconsequentialist distinction between harming as a means and harming as a side effect lacks an adequate rationale. Building on work by Frances Kamm and Stephen Darwall, I argue that there is such a rationale in terms of differential impact on relational equality between the victim and the beneficiary. Roughly, because nonconsensually harming A as a means of benefiting B subordinates A’s good to B’s good and harming as a side effect doesn’t do so, A has a stronger reason to reject principles that allow harming as a means. The problem isn’t, e.g., being forced to serve the agent’s goals, but rather being forced to serve others, which isn’t the case when one is harmed as a side effect. The legitimacy of such complaints is grounded in our authority to demand being treated as an equal rather than in the badness of harm. They are thus fundamentally second-personal. The broader lesson is that the fundamental disagreement between moral theories derives from their conception of the sources of reasons, which renders convergence unlikely.

618. A Defense of Affirmative Action for Conservatives
Jacobson, D. (2026). A Defense of Affirmative Action for Conservatives. PPA.
https://doi.org/10.1111/papa.70039
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The suggestion that academia should promote political diversity, implying as it does that the status quo is severely deficient, is commonly rejected on the grounds that to do so would require “affirmative action for conservatives.” That conclusion is typically treated as a reductio ad absurdum even by those who support conventional race‐ and sex‐based affirmative action. Nevertheless, this paper defends affirmative action for conservatives on both negative and positive grounds. It argues that since conservatives are discriminated against throughout academia, policies that benefit them in admissions and hiring would not be unjust to those who bear its burden. And it argues that the advantages of increased political diversity would accrue not just to those with heterodox opinions but would improve the intellectual atmosphere of academia at large. Hence, conservative affirmative action promotes the traditional academic mission and its norms of meritocracy, academic freedom, and the pursuit of knowledge; and it is not unjust to those with orthodox political views.

619. Can a Duty or a Right Be Held Disjunctively?
Cruft, R. (2026). Can a Duty or a Right Be Held Disjunctively?. Ratio Juris.
https://doi.org/10.1111/raju.70039
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper defends Matthew Kramer's novel proposal that sometimes an individually borne duty can be owed to correlative right‐holders disjunctively. It extends the proposal, suggesting that sometimes an individually held right entails correlative duties that are borne disjunctively. Drawing on a suggestion from Zofia Stemplowska, the paper argues that this extension of Kramer's proposal can be developed into a new solution to Onora O'Neill's famous claimability objection to natural human rights to goods and services. 620. Because They Are (Like) Us
                    : On Schopenhauer’s Pioneering Role in Animal Ethics and His Contribution to the Contemporary Debate
Köllen, T. (2026). Because They Are (Like) Us
                    : On Schopenhauer’s Pioneering Role in Animal Ethics and His Contribution to the Contemporary Debate. Moral Philosophy and Politics.
https://doi.org/10.1515/mopp-2026-0060
Abstract (original): Abstract Schopenhauer can be considered the first Western philosopher who, throughout all of his philosophical works, recognizes animals as a significant part of ethics. His notion of compassion ( Mitleid ) as the only basis for morally praiseworthy human conduct renders compassion for animals just as morally valuable as compassion for humans. Schopenhauer’s non-normative and non-prescriptive moral philosophy considers their will to life to be the key characteristic that makes humans and animals essentially the same. This allows us to bridge the human–animal divide without disputing potential differences in their abilities to reason, speak, or have interests, their capacity to feel pleasure or pain, or their sense of the future. For Schopenhauer, the origin of compassion and the impulse to act based on it do not spring from moral considerations, reasoning, or calculating, but from one’s own disposition ( Gesinnung ). His perspective therefore has strong parallels with many ethics of care approaches, without making care a moral imperative. In addition, Schopenhauer’s view of animals as “today’s slaves” creates an opportunity to find common ground between the opposing standpoints of contemporary political animal ethics. This includes the voices that often identify as liberationist/abolitionist and those that are often disparagingly labelled as only welfarist.

622. Enlightened Egoism and Its Discontents: Mapping the Business Case for Doing Good
Lynn, A. (2026). Enlightened Egoism and Its Discontents: Mapping the Business Case for Doing Good. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06512-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Various voices in business ethics and wider discussions of ethical capitalism endorse instrumental or strategic reasons for being ethical. Yet this orientation has at times eluded sustained philosophical engagement by the business ethics discipline. This article addresses this gap by mapping and critiquing what I term "enlightened egoism"—approaches that draw upon instrumental reasoning to derive normative guidance without recourse to duties, rights, values, or questions of the good. Careful reconstruction reveals that enlightened egoist arguments combine descriptive claims about how business operates with normative conclusions about how businesses ought to behave. Variations in these descriptive claims yield three distinct subtypes—evidential egoism, ends-enclosed egoism, and evolutionary egoism—each prominent in contemporary business ethics discourse. Examples are drawn from stakeholder theory, ESG investing (including hedge fund Engine No. 1's engagement with ExxonMobil), and diversity hiring initiatives. Through immanent critique, I demonstrate that enlightened egoism exhibits fundamental instabilities, specifically around indeterminacy in criteria for action, failure to account for heterogeneity, and conceptual slippage into determinist accounts of behavior. These limitations suggest the need for more rigorous integration of instrumental reasoning within—rather than as replacement for—normative ethical frameworks.

623. The Ethics of Employability: Accountability, Justice, and Responsibility in Racialised Graduate Labour Markets
Aziz, M.A. et al. (2026). The Ethics of Employability: Accountability, Justice, and Responsibility in Racialised Graduate Labour Markets. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06516-0
Abstract (original): Abstract Persistent racialised inequalities continue to shape graduate outcomes in the United Kingdom, despite decades of policy reform, institutional initiatives, and employability-focussed interventions. Racially minoritised graduates consistently experience ethnic penalties in hiring, earnings, and career progression, even when controlling for educational attainment and institutional prestige, with UK-born and international racially minoritised graduates facing structurally distinct but compounding forms of disadvantage. Yet, dominant employability frameworks have positioned employability as an individual responsibility, centred on skills, capitals, and adaptability, thereby obscuring the structural injustices embedded in higher education and labour markets. This paper reframes employability as an ethical domain of pressing importance for both higher education and business ethics. Drawing on an integrated theoretical architecture comprising normative foundations of justice (distributive, procedural, recognition, and epistemic), race-centred lenses (Critical Race Theory, intersectionality, and Bourdieu’s theory of capitals), an accountability spine, and the capabilities approach, it develops the Ethical Employability Framework (race-centred), which redistributes responsibility across universities, employers, and the state within the UK. The framework exposes how employability discourses can function as a form of “misframing”, legitimising inequality by shifting responsibility onto graduates while neglecting systemic exclusion. The paper’s theoretical contributions are hierarchically organised: it reframes employability as an ethical construct; integrates race as a constitutive theoretical concern through a race-centred explanatory architecture; develops an accountability spine that operationalises shared institutional responsibility; adopts the capabilities approach as the evaluative standard for substantive flourishing; and maps these elements into a coherent multi-level framework connecting normative theory to institutional practice. In doing so, the paper advances a justice-oriented agenda for employability scholarship, policy, and practice that places racially minoritised graduates at its centre.

631. An Unredeemed Promise: The Political Epistemology of Frankfurt School Critical Theory
Vogelmann, F. (2026). An Unredeemed Promise: The Political Epistemology of Frankfurt School Critical Theory. Constellations.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-8675.70092
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT However we define them, critical theories must fulfil the three conditions Max Horkheimer formulates in his seminal essay “Traditional and Critical Theory.” Critical theories must understand their own situation within contemporary society as constitutive of their theoretical practice and its results. Therefore, they must diagnose their social conditions, and do so not in a purely philosophical manner: they are necessitated to integrate empirical results of social research. Yet they also must spell out the philosophical implications of their own situatedness. Hence, just as they cannot be “pure” philosophy, they cannot be “pure” social research. Finally, critical theories must not aim only at an accurate understanding of their social conditions but also at overcoming them. In one way or another, they must aim at emancipation. If critical theories are “impure” by their very nature, they are forced to break with sovereign conceptions of epistemology. The necessity to intertwine epistemology and analysis of actually existing social conditions is built into critical theory's conceptual foundations. Non‐sovereign political epistemology is thus a requirement for and the promise of critical theories—a promise not often redeemed by critical theories and thus an important task for critical theories to come.

632. Constituent Rights and the Right to Strike
Björö, M. (2026). Constituent Rights and the Right to Strike. Constellations.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-8675.70094
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article asks what it would mean for a strike not merely to exercise, demand, or claim rights but to constitute a right through its own existence. Developing the concept of constituent rights in relation to the strike, I describe a mode of existence in which the collective power to act coincides with the right to act. The strike provides an especially useful site for these explorations: The withdrawal of labor complicates any straightforwardly affirmative approach, whereas the constituent relations formed through that withdrawal complicate a straightforwardly negative approach. I approach strikes through the methodological lens of the meanwhile, suspending their outcome as the criterion of their existence in order to attend to how the power to strike is continuously composed and decomposed through affective and transindividual relations. I argue, consequently, that constituent rights themselves may have a transindividual existence. If the right to strike can exist within those relations, however transiently, then the article shifts the question from whether radical politics should remain within or beyond the language of rights to a question of what counts as the existence of rights in the first place.

633. Defining a Characteristic Stimulus of a Disposition
Choi, S. (2026). Defining a Characteristic Stimulus of a Disposition. Ratio.
https://doi.org/10.1111/rati.70045
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The first step philosophers normally take when they discuss dispositions is to introduce the notions of a characteristic stimulus and manifestation of a disposition D. But we will see below that this very first step is fraught with pitfalls and problems. Though philosophers haven't given an explicit characterization of a characteristic stimulus of D, they implicitly appeal to the thought that a characteristic stimulus of D is an event‐type that typically serves as a proximate cause of D's manifestation. So understood, dispositions will be found to have far more characteristic stimuli than they are ordinarily thought to have. In this paper, however, I will present a characterization of a characteristic stimulus of D that is not only theoretically better motivated but delivers more commonsensical rulings in specific examples than the one that philosophers have implicitly accepted. I will go on to argue that, once this characterization is put in place, we can contain the wild proliferation of characteristic stimuli of dispositions.

634. Necessary and Sufficient Conditions Are Converse Relations
Silva, M.M. (2026). Necessary and Sufficient Conditions Are Converse Relations. Theoria.
https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70135
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT According to the so‐called ‘standard theory of conditions’, ‘being a sufficient condition of’ and ‘being a necessary condition of’ are converse relations, that is, if A is a sufficient condition for B , B is a necessary condition for A . This theory faces well‐known counterexamples that appeal to causal and other asymmetric considerations. I show that these counterexamples lose their plausibility once we clarify two key assumptions of the standard theory: that to satisfy a condition is to instantiate a property, and that what is usually called the ‘conditionship relation’ is an inferential relation. Throughout the paper, I favour this way of interpreting the standard theory over an alternative interpretation outlined in causal terms, since it can be applied to all the counterexamples without losing its intuitive appeal.

635. Devotion and Dialectical Invulnerability

Abstract (original): Abstract I focus on Katsafanas’s conceptions of devotion and dialectical invulnerability, including the kinds of reasons to which devotions are invulnerable. 637. Between corporate values and grandstanding: The compatibility of corporate activism with democracy
Frye, H. (2026). Between corporate values and grandstanding: The compatibility of corporate activism with democracy. Politics Philos Econ.
https://doi.org/10.1177/1470594x261506688
Abstract (original): Does corporate sociopolitical activism violate democratic principles? Critics argue that “woke capitalism” distorts public discourse and politicizes market life. This article focuses on a specific form of corporate sociopolitical activism: expressive activism, where businesses intervene in political discourse through social media posts, advertising campaigns, branding decisions, and the like. I argue that concerns with expressive corporate activism are overstated. To make this argument, I distinguish between integral activism, which reflects the core identity of a business, and incidental activism, which is opportunistic or disconnected from a firm's core values. For the former, such activism has expressive and potential epistemic benefits that can evade the worries surrounding equality and politicization. The upshot is that complaints about corporate activism focus on symptoms rather than causes of our democratic ills. Our complaints are better directed towards the mechanisms that produce political monocultures at the corporate level, damaging polarization, and excessive politicization.

640. Blame and redemption
Matheson, B. (2026). Blame and redemption. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02631-w
Abstract (original): Abstract What happens when a person redeems herself? According to interminability theorists, a redeemed person remains blameworthy but may cease to be an all-things-considered appropriate target of blame. According to many terminability theorists, however, a redeemed person ceases to be blameworthy. 641. Defending ontic structural realism in quantum field theory
Meyer, A. (2026). Defending ontic structural realism in quantum field theory. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02650-7
Abstract (original): Abstract Ontic structural realism (OSR) has been suggested as a metaphysics for quantum field theory (QFT). A common objection to OSR highlights the presence of fundamental intrinsic properties in physical theories (such as QFT) as undermining OSR. As there are purported examples of fundamental intrinsic properties possessed by fields in QFT, OSR is purportedly refuted in QFT (Berghofer, 2018). This article defends OSR from this argument. There are two possible strategies for responding to this argument: (1) denying that Berghofer’s counterexamples to OSR are intrinsic properties, and (2) finding a means of accommodating intrinsic properties in OSR. Additionally, as there are a number of means available for OSR to accommodate intrinsic properties, the second strategy also succeeds.

642. Clarifying the Structure of Current Debates on Mixed Affect: Ontologies of Emotion, Models of Valence, and the Scope of Kahneman’s ‘Vacillation Hypothesis’
Ombrato, M.D. (2026). Clarifying the Structure of Current Debates on Mixed Affect: Ontologies of Emotion, Models of Valence, and the Scope of Kahneman’s ‘Vacillation Hypothesis’. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10493-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Felt emotional ambivalence, bittersweet emotional experience, and other cases of apparent coexistence between affectively discordant emotions raise a much-debated empirical question: do affectively discordant feelings actually coexist (Coexistence hypothesis), or do they just seem to us to do so, due to their rapid alternation (Vacillation hypothesis)? This paper seeks to clarify what is at stake in addressing this question, without thereby endorsing any of the standard answers to it as the debate is usually framed; instead, it argues that coexistence and vacillation are best treated as distinct, context‑dependent mechanisms rather than rival global accounts of mixed affect. The analysis I propose reveals that the denial of Coexistence does not directly depend on commitment to ‘bipolar’ models of affective valence as is normally accepted, since the polar opposition of pleasure and unpleasure does not by itself exclude Coexistence. Rather, this denial stems from commitment to a particular ontology of emotions, namely, the view that occurrent emotions are partly constituted from a simple , unitary core affect state that, at any given instant, must bear either positive or negative valence. Having disentangled the Vacillation hypothesis from debates about the structure of valence, the paper further argues that recent psychology literature has misinterpreted Kahneman’s ‘vacillation hypothesis’, overgeneralizing it beyond its original theoretical context. Kahneman originally proposed Vacillation for specific cases involving commensurable evaluative comparisons (e.g., framing effects in economic decision-making), not as a general account of mixed emotional affect. The paper thus distinguishes between two mechanisms: vacillation, which applies when competing evaluations operate on a shared metric, and coexistence, which applies to cases in which emotion episodes are focused on distinct, incommensurable value-aspects of emotional situations.

649. Analytical Aristotelianism and Analytical Jurisprudence
Claeys, E.R. (2026). Analytical Aristotelianism and Analytical Jurisprudence. The American Journal of Jurisprudence.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ajj/auag026
Abstract (original): Abstract This essay, part of a symposium on New Natural Law and analytical Thomism, studies how teleological analytical methods have enriched analytical jurisprudence. Those “analytical Aristotelian” methods have inspired two promising arguments in support of the natural law thesis. The “legal point of view argument” holds that law cannot be analyzed adequately except from the viewpoint of the wise statesman. That argument applies to law Aristotle’s strategy of definition with central cases. The “artifact argument” holds that law has the same basic structure as other artifact kinds and that its most important feature is its function, its promoting the common good by general and impersonal rule. That argument applies to law Aristotle’s four-cause teleological framework. This essay summarizes the artifact and legal point of view arguments, and it identifies the Aristotelian features in both. The essay also explains the comparative advantages of each argument in analytical natural law theory. The artifact argument makes claims about law’s necessary features, while the legal point of view argument addresses methodological questions about how those features should be picked out. As made to date, however, neither argument addresses another preoccupation of analytical philosophy, explaining how shared concepts shape lay people’s social instincts in practice.

Abstract (original): Abstract Just as new natural lawyers and their rivals dispute, among other divisions, who follows in the intellectual legacy of St. Thomas Aquinas, they also argue about who rightly lays claim to the legacy of later Thomists, such as the analytic Thomists of the mid- and late twentieth century. On the subject of political and legal authority, the central tradition of natural law theory, encompassing both analytic Thomism and new natural law theory, rightly does not treat consent as a necessary condition. However, given the modern dominance of consent theories, this article begins by addressing the failures of those. The article then compares key writings on authority of the analytic Thomist Elizabeth Anscombe and the new natural lawyer John Finnis, and argues that, despite initial differences in approach and formulation, they ultimately have a shared understanding of the natural law justification of authority.

654. Non-Identity and Compensation for Climate Change: The Not-So-Minor Difference Claim
Düvel, E. & García-Portela, L. (2026). Non-Identity and Compensation for Climate Change: The Not-So-Minor Difference Claim. Utilitas.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0953820826100612
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper examines the implications of the Non-Identity Problem (NIP) for real-world climate change-related duties, responding to Lukas Tank’s Minor Difference Claim. Tank argues that robust climate action (involving mitigation, adaptation, and compensation) is already required to respond to the foreseeable negative effects on individuals whose identities are fixed, so accepting (or “biting the bullet” on) the NIP makes only a minor difference to our climate change-related duties. We challenge this view by arguing that accepting the NIP makes a not-so-minor difference for compensatory climate duties. First, it affects their extent. Second, it generates highly unequal compensatory claims both across individuals born at different times ( intragenerational difference ) and among those exposed to the same climate impacts at different times ( intertemporal difference ). We conclude that one must either accept these implications or develop a strategy to resolve or avoid the NIP.

655. Ambigüedad institucional: la enredada respuesta del Sistema interamericano al internamiento involuntario en situaciones de crisis psiquiátricas
Lazarte, R.A.B. & Caycho, R.A.C. (2026). Ambigüedad institucional: la enredada respuesta del Sistema interamericano al internamiento involuntario en situaciones de crisis psiquiátricas. International Journal of Constitutional Law (I-CON).
https://doi.org/10.1093/icon/moag110
Abstract (original): Abstract The involuntary confinement of persons with disabilities during psychiatric crises is one of the most intractable debates in international human rights law. The interpretation of article 14 of the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities has produced an impasse: whereas the CRPD Committee holds that commitment grounded in disability is prohibited, other bodies and courts continue to admit exceptions premised on risk. The Inter-American system has drawn remarkably little attention in this debate. This article maps the competing interpretations in international law and examines the Inter-American response through Guachalá Chimbó v. Ecuador. We argue that the Inter-American Commission and Court have generated an institutional ambiguity: they combined incompatible models —absolute prohibition, a risk-based exception, and a functional reading of consent— without acknowledging the underlying normative disagreement, thereby generating legal uncertainty.

656. Lesión deontológica del jurista: elección judicial y escisión entre sistema y rol en México
Gama, L. (2026). Lesión deontológica del jurista: elección judicial y escisión entre sistema y rol en México. International Journal of Constitutional Law (I-CON).
https://doi.org/10.1093/icon/moag111
Abstract (original): Abstract Institutional transformations of the judiciary may affect the conditions under which professional roles are performed. This article develops the concept of deontological injury to the legal professional, understood as the systematic subordination of role-constitutive reasons within professional deliberation. Drawing on H. L. A. Hart’s internal point of view and on J. Raz’s and J. C. Bayón’s accounts of normativity and reasons for action, it argues that acceptance of the legal system may persist even when role-based reasons lose their guiding position, thereby opening the possibility of a rupture between the normativity of the system and that of the professional role. When political or prudential reasons become functionally relevant to access to or continued tenure in judicial office, deontological injury may appear.

657. The Metaphysics of Homelands
Kovacs, D.M. (2026). The Metaphysics of Homelands. Mind.
https://doi.org/10.1093/mind/fzag053
Abstract (original): Abstract National self-determination has an inherently territorial dimension: people who live together in a shared polity require land that can host the population, whose resources can be extracted for the common good, and whose history they find meaningful. This paper explores a particular kind of land that is of distinguished importance in political philosophy: the homeland. Homelands are significant because of the role that is often attributed to them in justifying territorial rights. However, on reflection, the metaphysical nature of homelands is curious. Some of the platitudes we tend to hold about them imply that they resemble large material objects, while others suggest that we should treat them as space-time locations. In this paper, I consider several possible accounts of homelands that allow us to reconcile these intuitions with each other, ultimately coming down in favour of a conception of homelands as set-theoretic constructions. I combine this idea with a particular view of what turns a mere piece of land into a homeland, which I call the worked land view. The worked land view has far-ranging consequences not only for metaphysicians but for political philosophers as well: among other things, it helps us overcome a recalcitrant problem about self-determination referenda.

658. Scope 3 Emissions Framing and Corporate Climate Litigation: Encouraging the Right Narratives?
Hilson, C. (2026). Scope 3 Emissions Framing and Corporate Climate Litigation: Encouraging the Right Narratives?. OJLS.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ojls/gqag034
Abstract (original): Abstract The article analyses the framing of scope 3 emissions in corporate climate litigation and the narratives they give rise to. It examines scope 3’s origins and its presence in legislative and other regulatory measures before analysing scope 3 emissions framing in a series of landmark cases globally. The case law on scope 3 is presented across five broad categories involving corporations: business challenges to climate disclosure legislation, and then greenwashing, project, due diligence and liability cases brought by the climate movement. How do we and how should we frame what a company’s emissions are, for which it is legally responsible? The article outlines how the case law reflects a framing contest over what emissions properly belong to a company. It then discusses narratives involved in some of the key scope 3 emissions categories. While finding that there are strategically positive narratives for the climate movement, it argues that some of its corporate climate litigation plays into problematic private governance and market-led narratives. A case is made for a carbon takeback obligation (CTBO) as part of a policy-led narrative to help resolve these tensions.

659. Standing duties over time: Operation-bounded responsibility and historical injustice
Hou, W. (2026). Standing duties over time: Operation-bounded responsibility and historical injustice. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag084
Abstract (original): Abstract Some duties are completed by the performance they require. Others remain live because their defining aim remains unrealized. I argue that standing duties of active repair are of the second kind. Their force is not fixed by the permanent fact of a past wrong, nor are they discharged by any discrete act of apology, compensation, or settlement as such. That force varies with the state of the injury they aim to undo. Historical injustice is the hard case: the wrong does not lapse, but its continuing operation in present social relations may deepen, weaken, fall dormant, or cease. I call this view operation-bounded responsibility. It explains how reparative duties can be bounded in their active demands, and in principle completed, without treating the wrong as morally extinguished; and it identifies how a historical wrong fixes the content, the claimants, and the answerability of what is now owed.

660. From Nosography to Biography: A Four‐Level Framework for Evaluating Irremediability in Medical Assistance in Dying for Mental Health
Ramos‐Pozón, S. & Poveda‐Moral, S. (2026). From Nosography to Biography: A Four‐Level Framework for Evaluating Irremediability in Medical Assistance in Dying for Mental Health. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70215
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The assessment of “irremediability” in mental health constitutes the most complex bioethical challenge within medical assistance in dying laws, given the structural uncertainty inherent in psychiatry. This article proposes a four‐level analytical framework designed to transform diagnostic ambiguity into a prudent and auditable decision. In the first level (technical), “incurability” is delimited as a nosographic judgment based on treatment futility and traceability, as opposed to “irremediability” as a clinical‐pragmatic judgment. The second level (phenomenological) defines irremediability as a ‘frozen temporality’ and a closure of the biographical horizon, where suffering becomes structural. The third level (ethical) advocates for “relational autonomy” and the two‐track approach as safeguards against both therapeutic obstinacy and abandonment, ensuring that intensive rehabilitation coexists with the request. Finally, the fourth level (legal) analyzes how certain euthanasia legislations integrate biographical functionality, moving beyond a purely biological paradigm. It is concluded that the transition from a nosographic model to a biographical one allows for the respect of individual autonomy and life history without abdicating clinical responsibility, offering a decision‐making standard that harmonizes technical rigor with compassion.

661. ‘Chucking their stuff into ChatGPT and hitting send’: AI slop in the administrative state
MEERS, J. (2026). ‘Chucking their stuff into ChatGPT and hitting send’: AI slop in the administrative state. J Law Soc.
https://doi.org/10.1111/jols.70084
Abstract (original): Abstract Most research on artificial intelligence (AI) in the administrative state focuses on its use by officials. This article addresses a comparatively neglected issue: the impact of the use of AI tools by the public themselves. Drawing on a qualitative survey with over 500 civil servants in the United Kingdom, it argues that AI use by the public is increasingly disrupting the front line of the administrative state. The argument is set out in three parts. First, as the administrative state increasingly relies on text‐based inputs, officials use correspondence as a proxy for individuals – a longstanding assumption of ‘autographic’ authorship that the diffusion of generative AI tools unsettles. Second, the problem can be characterized as ‘AI slop’: AI‐generated correspondence with high volume and velocity that adopts a particular voice in interactions with officials. Third, this AI slop causes three forms of disruption: to the signals that officials draw from correspondence, to trust between officials and the public, and to systems that are ill‐equipped to deal with it. The article concludes by arguing that the status quo itself fails to adequately meet the needs of many people accessing services, and that systems need to be redesigned not only for a society beset by AI slop, but also in ways that address the drivers of it.

662. To trust or not to trust? The epistemic and moral dimensions of distrust in Italian commercial mediations
MINISSALE, A. (2026). To trust or not to trust? The epistemic and moral dimensions of distrust in Italian commercial mediations. J Law Soc.
https://doi.org/10.1111/jols.70085
Abstract (original): Abstract This article challenges enduring legal and economic ideals that portray commercial disputes as based solely on financial demands and governed by rational calculation devoid of emotions. Drawing on ethnographic observations of commercial mediations in Italy, it shows how distrust shapes decision making and interaction in individual–business as well as business–business conflicts. Combining sociological perspectives on epistemic and moral emotions with theories of status and power, the article conceptualizes distrust as an epistemic‐moral emotion that shapes how disputants interpret facts, assign blame, and evaluate future cooperation. The analysis demonstrates that in individual–business mediations distrust is expressed through narratives of suffering and moral outrage that demand empathic recognition and moral validation, whereas in business–business mediations distrust centres on perceived violations of professional integrity, credibility, and prestige, prompting status defence and power reassertion. In this way, the article contributes to socio‐legal studies on mediation, economic interaction, and decision making.

664. Narrative power and justice: computational insights into Indigenous–state consultation dynamics
LEVINE‐SCHNUR, R. et al. (2026). Narrative power and justice: computational insights into Indigenous–state consultation dynamics. J Law Soc.
https://doi.org/10.1111/jols.70090
Abstract (original): Abstract This article examines how narrative power shapes recognition and legitimacy in state‐led consultation procedures with Indigenous communities. Analysing two non‐binding consultations involving the Bedouin community in Israel, the study applies a voice‐centric computational methodology to over 200 oral testimonies. Using topic modelling and statistical analysis, it reveals a persistent narrative divide: Bedouin speakers foregrounded historical narratives of communal land possession with odds more than eight times higher than those of non‐Bedouin participants, while state actors relied predominantly on legalistic and procedural discourse oriented towards future governance. This divergence reflects competing conceptions of justice. For Indigenous participants, the past constituted the foundation of their claims, and the recognition of historical connection and dispossession was a prerequisite for negotiating future arrangements. The state, by contrast, largely bracketed history in favour of formal legal entitlements and procedural frameworks that operate to the detriment of Bedouin claims. An analysis of the Goldberg Commission's final report shows that this legalistic framing was ultimately adopted as the Commission's dominant narrative. By shifting analytical attention from speaking to listening, the article advances socio‐legal scholarship and offers methodological and policy insights for designing consultation processes that more meaningfully engage with and listen to Indigenous voices.

665. Quantitative Self‐Defeat Among the Never‐Liars: Kant, Augustine, and Aquinas
Sorensen, R. (2026). Quantitative Self‐Defeat Among the Never‐Liars: Kant, Augustine, and Aquinas. Metaphilosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/meta.70055
Abstract (original): Abstract Kant claims that every lie is as grave as perjury. Kant also believed in the opacity of the self. Even if under the impression of telling the truth, the testifier might be lying. Every declaration therefore incurs the risk of being gravely wrong. But instead of being a man of few words, Kant has a career total of two million words. Augustine and Aquinas also forbid all lies. These two prefigurers of Kant rank some lies as far less wrong than other lies. Augustine and Aquinas are also less pessimistic about authors' ability to detect their own lying. Nevertheless, the risk of grave lying remains too high to reconcile with the volubility of Augustine and Aquinas. These giants among the never‐liars exhibit a quantitative weakness that is also present among less prolific and more qualified prohibitors of lying, such as W. D. Ross.

666. Owing and Quantification
Sreenivasan, G. (2026). Owing and Quantification. Ratio Juris.
https://doi.org/10.1111/raju.70037
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper engages with Kramer's provocative analysis of disjunctive quantification. His idea is that someone can owe a duty to a disjunction of agents (and some such disjunction can together hold the correlative claim‐right). I find this idea very puzzling and I also see more difficulties than Kramer does in the proposition that a disjunction can pass his own test for right‐holding. I therefore offer the reader two ways to avoid having to rely on disjunctive quantification. 669. Decisions, Normative Powers, and Practical Situations
Vassiliou, A. (2026). Decisions, Normative Powers, and Practical Situations. Law Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10982-026-09601-7
Abstract (original): Abstract How do our decisions bear on our reasons for action? Raz holds that decision-making is a way of exercising our normative powers to create reasons for ourselves. Normative powers have been on the rise in moral and legal philosophy. Scanlon is an exception to this trend, holding that on source-externalism the normativity of decisions can be explained simply in virtue of the changes they can bring about in our practical situation. However, I argue that Scanlon’s account and Kolodny’s subsequent supplementation are wanting. In response, I propose a shift of attention to the deliberative conditions under which our earlier deciding self can consider the merits of a case compared to our future acting self. On this basis, I develop a new situational account that lies in the improvement to our future choice in combination with the costs of revisiting the issue. Hence, I conclude that normative powers are unnecessary for explaining the normativity of decisions.

672. Autoritarismo(s) en América Latina: Una visión granular
García, H.A. & Rincón, A.M. (2026). Autoritarismo(s) en América Latina: Una visión granular. International Journal of Constitutional Law (I-CON).
https://doi.org/10.1093/icon/moag117
Abstract (original): Abstract This article proposes a granular reading of contemporary authoritarianism in Latin America. Against frameworks that identify the phenomenon with open constitutional ruptures, already extensively identified and problematized in the literature, the article shows how authoritarianism frequently operates through law: through decrees, referenda, presidential vetoes, and constitutional reforms that progressively erode institutional checks without formally dismantling them. The article distinguishes between classical authoritarianism, focused on the erosion of civil and political rights, and extended authoritarianism, oriented toward insulating economic organization from democratic deliberation. 673. Improving Lives and Avoiding Harm
Campbell, T. & Kaczmarek, P. (2026). Improving Lives and Avoiding Harm. Noûs.
https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70074
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The Asymmetry in population ethics holds that creating a miserable person is morally impermissible, while not creating a happy person is morally permissible, all else being equal. Harm‐avoidance theories explain the Asymmetry by claiming that, in certain contexts, only actions resulting in harm are impermissible. Two problems threaten these theories: (i) The Problem of Improvable Life Avoidance—requiring agents to avoid creating improvable good lives—and (ii) requiring dominated options. Sophisticated harm‐avoidance theories avoid both problems. However, we show that any harm‐avoidance theory entails either (1) a worse form of The Problem of Improvable Life Avoidance, (2) a troubling form of sadism, (3) an implausibly extreme priority for avoiding a small harm for one person over greater harms for many people, or (4) spurious prohibition dilemmas. Furthermore, we argue that any reasonable theory of the Asymmetry entails at least one of (1)–(4).

674. Social Normativity Without Realism: A Fictionalist Challenge
Bakkal, E. (2026). Social Normativity Without Realism: A Fictionalist Challenge. Philosophy of the Social Sciences.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00483931261505199
Abstract (original): I challenge forms of social normativism that treat social normative entities—deontic statuses, deontic powers, and social norms—as indispensable to the explanation of action. I distinguish two roles these entities are said to play: causal and justificatory. Against the causal role, I argue that every causal explanation invoking social normative entities has a corresponding explanation that does not. Against the justificatory role, I argue that social scientists can adopt a methodological fictionalist stance toward justificatory explanations without thereby incurring ontological commitment. Realists therefore owe an account of why ontological commitment remains necessary for explanation.

677. Enforcing democratic norms through reciprocal transgressions
Lepoutre, M. (2026). Enforcing democratic norms through reciprocal transgressions. Politics Philos Econ.
https://doi.org/10.1177/1470594x261508090
Abstract (original): Contemporary democracies are increasingly threatened by political actors who transgress democratic norms to entrench their own power. When, if ever, is it democratically permissible to respond to such transgressions by reciprocating—that is, by deviating from democratic rules? A key concern is that this response risks triggering an escalating cycle of democratic transgressions, thereby severely eroding democratic norms. I argue that reciprocal deviations can instead play a central role in enforcing, and therefore preserving, democratic norms. Crucially, this enforcement rationale entails distinctive normative constraints on when, how, and by whom reciprocal deviations should be deployed—constraints which, I argue, diverge meaningfully from those recommended by other rationales for reciprocation. I then address two objections: that reciprocation is not needed to enforce democratic norms; and that my argument is overly idealized. While neither objection is decisive, each helps further specify the circumstances under which the present defense applies.

679. The Live Possibility Model of Disinformation: A Non-doxastic Account
Palmqvist, C. (2026). The Live Possibility Model of Disinformation: A Non-doxastic Account. Erkenntnis.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10670-026-01186-w
Abstract (original): Abstract In the philosophical literature, it is often assumed that disinformation leads to false beliefs. However, empirical work in the social sciences has established that much disinformation rather creates uncertainty. Taking seriously these empirical findings, I develop a non-doxastic theory of disinformation which I call the “live possibility model”. The core claim of my theory is that disinformation introduces live possibilities the victim cannot reject. Live possibilities are epistemic possibilities which are likely enough to be relevant for thought and action. When a live possibility is introduced, it forces a suspension of judgement. In the resulting context of uncertainty, the subject navigates by use of non-doxastic attitudes, such as hope, fear, faith, etc. Unlike most other philosophical accounts of disinformation, this model does not ascribe irrationality to disinformed subjects. Instead, it suggests that disinformation exploits processes of critical thinking. While there have been some other recent philosophical attempts to understand disinformation in terms of uncertainty—due to Blake-Turner, Harris, and Rini—I demonstrate how the live possibility model incorporates their insights while surpassing them theoretically.
