685. Sensation and Perception in Merleau‐Ponty
Antich, P. (2026). Sensation and Perception in Merleau‐Ponty. European Journal of Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/ejop.70132
Abstract (original): Abstract The long “Introduction” to the Phenomenology of Perception begins with a rejection of the classical notion of sensation as “the most confused notion there is.” And yet Merleau‐Ponty continues to speak of sensation throughout the Phenomenology , and indeed, throughout his corpus. This poses an obvious interpretive puzzle, and one which has not received a focal treatment in the literature. In this paper, I consider three questions: 1) What view of sensing does Merleau‐Ponty reject in the Introduction? 2) On the other hand, what view of sensation does he wish to maintain? 3) How exactly does sensation, on his view, differ from perception? By working through these questions, I propose an interpretation on which sensation is conceived as a kind of perception, and one which is expressively basic (in a sense to be explained), such that in being given, it discloses other phenomena that are not properly given.

686. Legal pluralism in Ecuador: gender‐based violence, Indigenous women, and processes of dispute resolution
                    *
FIALLO, L. (2026). Legal pluralism in Ecuador: gender‐based violence, Indigenous women, and processes of dispute resolution
                    *. J Law Soc.
https://doi.org/10.1111/jols.70086
Abstract (original): Abstract This article examines legal pluralism in Ecuador between 2007 and 2017. By theorizing legal pluralism as a regime, I analyse the articulation between its institutional design, formal and informal rules of coordination between jurisdictions, and actors’ legal strategies, discourses, and practices in gender‐based violence cases against Indigenous women. The study draws on documentary analysis of seven judicial files involving the activation of both the ordinary jurisdiction and the Indigenous jurisdiction that led to a jurisdictional conflict, alongside 43 interviews with Indigenous authorities, women leaders, victims, judicial officials, and policymakers. Rules governing legal pluralism enabled asymmetric legal interactions and jurisdictional disputes through which local power was contested. Indigenous women faced a choice between two constrained pathways: community processes reproducing discrimination or punitive state frameworks limiting transformative reparations. These findings suggest that strategic legal mobilization reshaped institutional landscapes while reproducing colonial formations. Conceptualizing legal pluralism as a regime shifts the focus beyond state‐centred accounts, foregrounding everyday negotiations and power relations.

687. Trauma‐informed lawyering in practice: an inverted lens on independent legal advice and representation for complainants in sexual violence cases
KINGHAN, J. et al. (2026). Trauma‐informed lawyering in practice: an inverted lens on independent legal advice and representation for complainants in sexual violence cases. J Law Soc.
https://doi.org/10.1111/jols.70087
Abstract (original): Abstract This article examines the implementation of trauma‐informed lawyering within models of independent legal advice (ILA) and independent legal representation (ILR) for complainants in sexual violence cases. Drawing on an international qualitative study at a time of significant reform across several jurisdictions, it identifies the key features of trauma‐informed practice in this context and explores its less visible effects on lawyers and the criminal justice system. Using Cyrus Tata's concept of ‘inverting the lens’, the article highlights the emotional demands placed on lawyers, including the risk of vicarious trauma, and examines the institutional constraints that shape the delivery of trauma‐informed ILA and ILR. While trauma‐informed lawyering can enhance complainant agency and mitigate some of the harms associated with the adversarial process, it cannot resolve systemic problems such as delays, underfunding, and marginalization. The article argues that while trauma‐informed ILA and ILR represent an important intervention, they may also risk legitimizing a criminal justice system that remains structurally flawed.

688. Reconceptualizing access to justice for social welfare legal problems: a relational framework

https://doi.org/10.1111/jols.70088
Abstract (original): Abstract This article reconceptualizes access to justice for social welfare legal problems through a relational framework, foregrounding interactions between formal advice services, informal community organizations, and the social networks through which people navigate everyday problems. The findings demonstrate that access to justice is shaped by the quality of bridging and linking relationships within communities, alongside the effectiveness of formal advice provision. 690. Welfare’s Role in the Mechanics of Claims: Reply to Sinclair and Yuan
Walen, A. (2026). Welfare’s Role in the Mechanics of Claims: Reply to Sinclair and Yuan. J Moral Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1163/17455243-bja10196
Abstract (original): Abstract The mechanics of claims (MOC) provides a framework for taking people’s pro tanto rights, or claims, into account and determining who has a right to do what or to get what treatment from others. It is grounded on three principles: the Autonomy Principle, the Equality Principle, and the Welfare Principle. Thomas Sinclair and Yuan Yuan argue in different ways that the Welfare Principle raises problems for the plausibility of the MOC. Sinclair highlights an important distinction between two kinds of permissions agents can have to act: clean permissions that require no justifications and carry no moral residue, and conflict permissions that require justifications and do carry a moral residue. He points out that the Welfare Principle makes it hard to see how these can be distinguished. Yuan argues that the Welfare Principle makes it seem that property rights are quite unreliable, and that the occasions for unilateral enforcement of rights are too pervasive under the MOC. In response to Sinclair, I argue that we can distinguish clean from conflict permissions by paying attention to agent claims not to have to serve the welfare of others. And in response to Yuan, I argue that property rights are actually about as reliable as they should be on the MOC, and that it does not too often allow for unilateral enforcement of rights.

691. Idealized Models, Counterfactual Reasoning, and Inductive Risk in Social Science Contexts: The Case of Minimal Models for Inequity
Marino, P. (2026). Idealized Models, Counterfactual Reasoning, and Inductive Risk in Social Science Contexts: The Case of Minimal Models for Inequity. Philosophy of the Social Sciences.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00483931261505202
Abstract (original): This project explores the roles that non-epistemic values play in the use of highly idealized models in social science contexts. Focusing on the use of bargaining models for predictions about interventions, I argue that epistemological challenges for such inferences highlight the need for inductive risk assessments: we must normatively evaluate the consequences of over-confidence and under-confidence in the relevant conclusions. However, I also show that evaluating these consequences is complex, so that inferences from these models to predictive inferences may be difficult to support. I then consider broader implications for recent research on the epistemic risks of using models.

692. “The S(trip Club) Got Something to Say!”: Adrienne Raquel's
                    ONYX
                    and the female gaze

                    ONYX
                    and the female gaze. Feminist Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14647001261490884
Abstract (original): As one of Houston's most famous strip clubs, Club Onyx has gained notoriety as one of the bastions of the Black South's erotic culture and hip-hop scene. 693. Re-reading Justice as Fairness Without Market-Based Assumptions
Craig, G. (2026). Re-reading Justice as Fairness Without Market-Based Assumptions. Res Publica.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11158-026-09803-9
Abstract (original): Abstract In A Theory of Justice , the principles of justice as fairness are selected via the original position. In the original position parties only know minimal, highly ‘general facts’ about human society, psychology, and politics, including principles of economic theory. While admitting ‘general facts’ of economic theory looks innocuous, which facts count matters. In the end Rawls permits only market-based regimes—property-owning democracy and liberal socialism—as possible just economic orders. However, this narrowing of the option set does not follow Rawls’s own procedure, admitting order-specific assumptions into the original position and across a procedure that is supposed to remain abstract. Moreover, when these assumptions filter into the original position, justice as fairness becomes a framework for arranging and regulating market-based institutions, rather than a test for a just economic order. If the original position and four-stage sequence were applied as Rawls originally described—open to institutional form under certain constraints—then Rawls’s procedure does not logically entail market-based systems but constrains them. If justice as fairness is to provide more than just conceptual and normative resources for evaluating market-based orders, my critique provides insight for where to begin, and that is with Rawls’s economic assumptions.

694. Humpty Dumpty Epistocracies: Democratic Inclusion, Public Ignorance and the Structural Instability of Epistocracy
Williams, R.B. (2026). Humpty Dumpty Epistocracies: Democratic Inclusion, Public Ignorance and the Structural Instability of Epistocracy. Res Publica.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11158-026-09805-7
Abstract (original): Abstract Epistocrats argue that public ignorance justifies epistocratic interferences with our political decisions to protect and promote the common good. In response, this paper shows that inclusive democracy is a more structurally stable institutional response to public ignorance than exclusionary epistocracy, whether justified with instrumentalist, paternalistic or jury defences. A Robust Political Economy framework helps to show why epistocracy is fragile. Epistocrats see that informed knowers are competent, but overlook that they are highly unrepresentative, which makes empowering them structurally unstable. An ignorant public does not know that the knowers are competent and lacks a peaceful way to oust unrepresentative politicians from power. This creates a competence/stability dilemma: either epistocracy prioritises a competent politics that fails to represent ignorant citizens and compromises stability, or epistocracy prioritises a stable politics that represents ignorant citizens but compromises the competence that epistocracy promises. This turns the democracy/epistocracy debate away from competence and towards institutional stability and feasibility. The problem is not that the public is too ignorant for democracy, but that the public may be too ignorant for epistocracy.

701. Natural Law and Judicial Power
Ekins, R. (2026). Natural Law and Judicial Power. The American Journal of Jurisprudence.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ajj/auag025
Abstract (original): Abstract This article considers the reasons for establishing judicial power and for structuring and exercising it reasonably. Natural law calls for institutions with authority to make and change law, and with authority to resolve disputes about this positive law. The ruler should found courts and differentiate judicial power, making provision for fair adjudication and for the rule of law. In adjudicating disputes, courts will change the legal position of the parties. Some judgments may change the general law, but there is good reason for courts to eschew general lawmaking responsibility. Courts should interpret enactments consistently with the intention of the lawmaker, a responsibility that is compatible with equitable interpretation. Natural law bears on how courts should be structured and the conditions under which judicial power should be exercised. Natural law does not confer on courts a general jurisdiction to adjudicate disputes about the constitution or to invalidate unjust laws. While natural law affirms human rights, it does not require human rights law, which may implicate courts in making law that threatens the common good.

702. Aristotle on the Boundaries of Processes
Zhou, L. (2026). Aristotle on the Boundaries of Processes. Mind.
https://doi.org/10.1093/mind/fzag057
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper challenges the dominant reading of Aristotle’s theory of the boundaries of processes, according to which he takes every interval of process to be ‘open’, excluding both the beginning and the end. Drawing on the Physics and Metaphysics, I argue that this reading is incorrect. Instead, Aristotle holds that every interval of process is half-open, excluding the end but including the beginning. He also extends this structure to intervals of rest because in both cases the end can (and must) exist without the earlier condition continuing, whereas the beginning cannot exist without the condition it initiates. The alternative reading helps improve our understanding of Aristotle’s handling of the well-known stopping and starting problem, and secures him a defensible place in both ancient and modern debates about boundaries.

704. Referring without a single target: Demonstratives, intentionalism, and partial intentions
Lee, S. (2026). Referring without a single target: Demonstratives, intentionalism, and partial intentions. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag088
Abstract (original): Abstract Intentionalism holds that the referent of a demonstrative is determined, at least in part, by the speaker’s referential intention. Although the view leaves open what kind of intention is required, it is natural to assume that successfully referring to a particular object requires intending to refer to that very object. This paper challenges this assumption and develops a weaker intentionalist account that permits demonstrative reference without a single target fixed in advance. Drawing on considerations from action theory, particularly concerning the Simple View, I introduce cases in which speakers appear to secure a referent despite being open to—but not unsettled about—multiple courses of action. Next, I address objections before considering a potential solution based on Jeffrey King’s analysis of felicitous underspecification. Finally, I propose an alternative account in terms of partial intentions and discuss its relation to the broader perspective that weaker referential pro-attitudes may suffice for demonstrative reference.

705. Risks of Substituting Epigenetic Age for Chronological Age in Medicine and Policy
Wohns, N. (2026). Risks of Substituting Epigenetic Age for Chronological Age in Medicine and Policy. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70213
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Epigenetic clocks—statistical models that estimate biological age from DNA methylation patterns—are increasingly used or proposed for use in both biomedical and non‐biomedical contexts. This article argues that the use of epigenetic clocks in these contexts introduces specific ethical problems. Three sets of problems are identified. The first concerns the measure itself: epigenetic age is a model‐dependent estimate of a contested concept, and treating it as a policy target risks decoupling it from its intended outcomes. The second concerns its substitution for chronological age: biological testing introduces risks of temporal instability, differential accuracy across demographic groups, expanded inferential scope beyond what individuals have consented to have assessed, and unequal access to score‐improving interventions. The third and deepest concern is structural inequity. Epigenetic clocks accurately capture the biological effects of social disadvantage, and policies that impose further burdens on structurally disadvantaged groups using epigenetic age risk entrenching that disadvantage under the guise of scientific objectivity. The analysis concludes that defensible use requires population‐specific validation, transparency about inferential scope, and prior assessment of distributional effects on disadvantaged groups.

706. I Do Not Want to Know How Long I Have: Prognostic Refusal, Temporal Framing, and the Right to Leave the Future Open
Matsui, M. (2026). I Do Not Want to Know How Long I Have: Prognostic Refusal, Temporal Framing, and the Right to Leave the Future Open. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70217
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Prognostic communication is commonly justified by its contribution to autonomous decision‐making, treatment planning, advance care planning, and preparation for death. Yet patients vary substantially in how much prognostic information they wish to receive. This paper identifies a distinct reason for prognostic refusal: a patient may decline further temporal specification not because death is denied or the truth is intolerable, but because prognosis can reorganize the future as a bounded remainder to be managed. I call this transformation the remaining‐life project . Awareness that illness is incurable or life‐limiting may already narrow the temporal horizon, while numerical or otherwise localized estimates can further sharpen and stabilize that horizon. Activities, relationships, unfinished affairs, and even inactivity may then become interpretable as uses of scarce remaining time. The paper distinguishes decision‐enabling information , which may be necessary for particular choices, from life‐framing information , which organizes life more broadly around an anticipated endpoint. It argues that prognostic refusal can be compatible with autonomy, agency, and authenticity, and defends a limited, defeasible authority to decline non‐essential temporal framing rather than an absolute right to ignorance. Because prognostic estimates are both uncertain and potentially life‐organizing, the appropriate clinical response is neither routine disclosure nor silence, but permission‐sensitive communication : offering prognostic discussion, clarifying preferences, providing what is necessary for present decisions, and avoiding unnecessary imposition of a temporal horizon.

707. Ignorance and Indifference of the Deaths of (Select) Others
Dror, L. (2026). Ignorance and Indifference of the Deaths of (Select) Others. PPA.
https://doi.org/10.1111/papa.70038
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This paper offers an explanation of the disparate concern Westerners have for civilians who die in different wars abroad. I consider two attempts to explain the disparate concern in terms of racism: first a more interpersonal understanding of racism as racist animus, and then a more structural explanation in terms of “white ignorance.” I argue that trying to understand the disparate concern evinced for different civilian casualties primarily in terms of racism is mistaken, and that a Marxist account of imperialism offers a plausible framework for explaining Western attitudes. The paper also argues for the interplay between imperialism and race, and for the philosophical importance of considering the role of imperialism and capitalism when considering race, racism, and white ignorance.

708. The Problem of the Infinite Regress of Ends
Cho, K. (2026). The Problem of the Infinite Regress of Ends. Ratio.
https://doi.org/10.1111/rati.70049
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This paper articulates the Problem of the Infinite Regress of Ends , a neglected difficulty in the theory of practical reason. Philosophers commonly hold that rational action must be justified by an end as its reason. Yet for that end to function as a reason, it seems that the end itself must be justified by a further end as its reason, giving rise to an apparent regress. I argue that this regress results from two seemingly compelling principles: No Arbitrary Ends and No Circular Ends . Each principle is required by the nature of practical reasoning, but together they entail that there must be an infinite regress of ends in the course of action justification. However, I show that the very nature of practical reasoning also implies that the order of action justification cannot extend infinitely. Thus another principle, No Infinite Regress of Ends , holds, and it is inconsistent with No Circular Ends and No Arbitrary Ends. Furthermore, the same underlying structure that supports No Infinite Regress of Ends also supports No Arbitrary Ends and No Circular Ends. The resulting trilemma reveals that any adequate account of practical reason must explain how justification can terminate without arbitrariness or circularity.

725. The scientist’s AI-pprentice: how open science shapes biosecurity risks by generating capabilities of AI tools in life science research
Rothweiler, E.M. et al. (2026). The scientist’s AI-pprentice: how open science shapes biosecurity risks by generating capabilities of AI tools in life science research. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01518-0
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence (AI) is becoming increasingly incorporated in the life sciences. Until recently, the need for data governance was masked by the limits of our human ability to aggregate, synthesise, and draw inferences from data. AI enhances not only speed of research and data processing, but also dual-use risks from training AI models with open access data. Concerns about AI-enabled chemical and biological weapons development become louder while governments, institutions and model developers scramble to mitigate and contain those risks. Data remains a crucial ingredient for AI model advancement, consequentially the lack of data governance of AI in life sciences leaves us collectively and personally vulnerable. Traditional data governance controls storage, transfer and access to datasets, but AI-enabled life science research raises a different issue: which novel capabilities emerge when models aggregate and orchestrate openly available data and tools, arising from individually benign contributions and not covered by current governance. Thus, we highlight questions and ethical considerations on which precautions are required when risks arise through data aggregation and re-use, how responsibility can be distributed, whether governance should focus on data or novel capabilities and if openness should cease to be default. Data governance works upstream, before data is introduced to an AI model. It follows that more life science dual-use data of concern should be held in Trusted Research Environments rather than released openly, and that dual-use data assessments become routine, implemented through research funders as chokepoints, to bridge the legislative gap until international dual-use standards are established.

726. Ethical and practical challenges of artificial intelligence for sleep and mental health in disability care: a narrative review
Alara, J.A. & Alara, O.R. (2026). Ethical and practical challenges of artificial intelligence for sleep and mental health in disability care: a narrative review. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01520-6
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence (AI) is increasingly being explored to support sleep and mental health care, yet its role in disability care remains insufficiently synthesised. This narrative review examines the ethical and practical challenges associated with AI applications for sleep and mental health in disability care, with attention to both people with disability and disability support workers. Current applications include wearable and sensor-based sleep monitoring, mood tracking systems, conversational agents, predictive models, and workforce-facing decision-support tools. These technologies may improve early detection, continuous monitoring, personalised support, and care coordination. However, the literature identifies substantial barriers to safe and equitable implementation. Practical challenges include poor accessibility, limited inclusive usability, workflow disruption, variable digital literacy, weak external validation, and concerns about reliability across diverse disability groups. Ethical concerns include privacy and data governance, meaningful informed consent, algorithmic bias, lack of transparency, intrusive surveillance, and the risk of dehumanising care. The evidence base is also limited by overreliance on general mental health AI literature, minimal focus on disability support workers, a lack of longitudinal and implementation research, and insufficient co-design with people with disability. Overall, AI may offer meaningful benefits in disability care, but responsible implementation requires disability-specific evidence, inclusive design, strong governance, human oversight, and sustained commitment to person-centred and rights-based care.

727. Values as dynamic priors: neurobiological constraints on formal models of value alignment
Bhattacharya, J. et al. (2026). Values as dynamic priors: neurobiological constraints on formal models of value alignment. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01528-y
Abstract (original): Abstract Formal models of human values have brought much-needed rigor to AI alignment research by making value relations explicit and evaluable by machine. Yet the assumptions that make such models computationally tractable may limit their ability to capture how values are represented, regulated, and expressed in human cognition. Drawing on evidence from neuroscience and the predictive processing framework, we examine three assumptions common to many formal value models. First, value importance is typically treated as fixed within a given context, whereas neurobiological evidence suggests it is dynamically shaped by arousal, uncertainty, and learning. Second, hierarchical value taxonomies assume that values can be fully represented through grounding components, yet some higher-level values, including dignity, integrity, and authenticity, appear difficult to evaluate through weighted aggregation alone. Third, formal models typically restrict value influence to the evaluation of actions, whereas neurobiological evidence suggests that values also influence attention, perception, and the interpretation of situations before explicit evaluation begins. We argue that these observations do not undermine formal value modeling but rather identify its limits and opportunities for extension. Specifically, we propose representing uncertainty about value importance, and its change over time, through distributional estimates, distinguishing compositional from non-compositional values, extending value influence to the construction of situation representations, and incorporating bottom-up learning into value taxonomy development. More broadly, we argue that engagement with neuroscience can help distinguish productive simplifications from potentially limiting assumptions, providing a more empirically grounded basis for the development of value-aligned AI systems.

731. The role of courts in the accommodation of national pluralism
Tirapu-Sanuy, J.M. (2026). The role of courts in the accommodation of national pluralism. International Journal of Constitutional Law (I-CON).
https://doi.org/10.1093/icon/moag128
Abstract (original): Abstract This article advances a constitutional theory of the judicial role in the accommodation of national pluralism—that is, a theory of how courts should respond to conflicts concerning minority nations, focusing on Quebec, Scotland, and Catalonia. It begins by proposing an account of plurinational federalism, understood as the application of the federal principle for the purpose of accommodating minority nations. The article then derives its implications for the judicial role. Ultimately, it argues that, when adjudicating territorial conflicts, courts should take national pluralism seriously to preserve and promote the ongoing legitimacy of the constitutional order. While both courts and political actors have a role to play in accommodating national pluralism, the judicial role becomes particularly important when political accommodation fails, especially in the context of rigid constitutions. The comparison highlights the strengths of the approach adopted in the Quebec Secession Reference and shows why the failure of courts to adopt a similar approach proved more problematic in Spain, with its rigid Constitution, than in the United Kingdom, where the constitution is flexible.

734. Deliberating and Reflecting on Relational Values: How Agent-Relative Biodiversity Values Can Be Justified and Criticized
Deplazes-Zemp, A. (2026). Deliberating and Reflecting on Relational Values: How Agent-Relative Biodiversity Values Can Be Justified and Criticized. The Monist.
https://doi.org/10.1093/monist/onag027
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT In this paper, I argue that Relational Values can support an argumentative defense of biodiversity in spite of their context-sensitivity and agent-relativity. Building on the work of several authors, I adopt a ‘deliberative buck-passing account of value’, according to which values do not themselves provide normative reasons for actions or attitudes. Rather, values refer to descriptive and evaluative properties of the object that provide such reasons. On this view, environmental Relational Values refer to extrinsic properties that can be critically examined for their normative force through general concepts and principles associated with human-nature relationships. Therefore, reasoning about Relational Values in collective deliberation and in personal reflection can provide a basis for justifying and criticizing the Relational Values held by oneself and others. Four examples of Relational Values associated with biodiversity are used to illustrate how such values can be shared, supported, contested, and rejected.

736. Evaluating the Decolonial Category of “Exteriority”: Reconsidering Globalization

Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Populist resentment is ostensibly rooted in elite misrecognition of the virtues of the ordinary people. 739. Two Persons, One Womb: Testing the Social Construction of Reproduction in Conjoined Twins
Villalba, A. (2026). Two Persons, One Womb: Testing the Social Construction of Reproduction in Conjoined Twins. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70218
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Standard models of reproductive autonomy presuppose a singular and bounded subject. This paper challenges that assumption by analysing the limit case of dicephalus parapagus conjoined twins where two distinct persons share a single reproductive system. I argue that this physiological configuration creates an ontological indeterminacy that biological essentialism cannot resolve. Drawing on a previous constructivist framework of reproduction, I demonstrate that when gestation is a shared biological event but personhood is distinct, the designation of “mother” cannot be discovered in the tissue but must be acknowledged through intention. The analysis proceeds to examine the severe ethical conflict arising when one twin wishes to reproduce while the other does not. I contend that prioritizing the reproductive desire of one sister over the refusal of the other constitutes a violation of bodily integrity analogous to involuntary gestational surrogacy. Consequently, I argue that in shared bodies, the non‐reproducing person must hold an absolute power of veto. This aligns with established bioethical precedents, such as those discussed by Robertson, where the right to avoid involuntary biological parenthood is often held to supersede a former partner's desire to gestate shared embryos. Finally, this paper posits that the principles derived from this extreme case offer a necessary heuristic for broader bioethical issues. The example of conjoined twins validates the distributed maternity of Reciprocal IVF in lesbian couples while simultaneously reinforcing the protections against coercive surrogacy. By deconstructing the most extreme form of shared biology, this paper offers an exploration of a case of fragmented maternity and its implications.

741. Doing It for the Plot
Baehni, A. (2026). Doing It for the Plot. J Soc Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1111/josp.70074
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The phrase “Do it for the plot” was recently coined by the actress and influencer Serena Kerrigan in a viral TikTok video where she urged viewers to remember that “if something works out, great, and if it doesn't, it's for the plot.” She explained that: “Do it for the plot” is an extension of the idea of being the main character of one's life (…). I think that people just get pretty upset when things don't go their way, or they are embarrassed if they got drunk one night and I'm like “its for the plot (…).” In this paper, I take Kerrigan's suggestion seriously and explore it as an idea worthy of philosophical investigation. In doing so, I examine a category of actions and conducts that has been overlooked by philosophers, as well as the reasons we might have for performing them. 742. Ecological Digital Democracy: Digitalisation and the Contestation of Ecological Democracy
Keary, M. (2026). Ecological Digital Democracy: Digitalisation and the Contestation of Ecological Democracy. Theoria.
https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70130
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Is it possible to imagine a digital ecological democracy? There are reasons it may not be. Chief among them is the environmental unsustainability of the existing digital transition. Despite this, green political theory (ecologism) cannot dismiss digitalisation: the current digital trajectory is not inevitable and its possibilities bear on the theory's central commitments. This article asks what digitalisation means for green political theory by examining its implications for the debate over ecological democracy. It contrasts the deliberative model advanced by Eckersley with the radical democratic alternatives proposed by Machin and Lepori, arguing that digitalisation exacerbates the weaknesses of the latter while offering, in reimagined form, resources for the former. In particular, Landemore's model of digital deliberative democracy is explored as a means of addressing the practical constraints that have long beset ecological deliberative theory. Digitalisation thus alters the balance of argument in ecologism. The article concludes that while digitalisation, as it stands, is neither democratic nor ecological, reconfigured forms of digitalisation may become normatively compelling within ecologism insofar as they make ecological democratic commitments more practicable. This capacity to reshape the terms of debate within green political theory warrants wider inquiry into digitalisation's implications across ecologism.

743. Why Knowledge Is First
Williamson, T. (2026). Why Knowledge Is First. Theoria.
https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70133
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This paper explains, from a broadly evolutionary perspective, why the category of knowledge is central to intelligent life, since the adaptive value of having a mind in the first place is to enable animals to adjust their actions flexibly and appropriately to a complex and rapidly changing environment, which in turn requires them to know what is going on in their environment. This is often a matter of life or death. Acting on knowledge is more adaptive than acting on belief. An intermediate hypothesis is that what matters is acting on true belief. This watering down loses explanatory value, although the category of belief can be understood in terms of the category of knowledge. The paper then turns from the priority of knowledge over belief to the priority of cognising in terms of knowledge over cognising in terms of belief. Many non‐human animals and very small children implicitly distinguish between what others know and what they don't know, but can't distinguish between what others believe and what they don't believe. This asymmetry is the opposite of what many epistemologists and other philosophers had assumed. It can be explained by considerations of efficiency, feasibility and heuristics in the psychological task of mindreading, in particular by an ‘open world’ default. (An application is made to the problematic role of common knowledge assumptions in some social scientific explanations.) Gettier cases of justified true belief without knowledge are often thought to show that the category of knowledge must be tricky and sophisticated. On the contrary, it is explained how very simple heuristics usable even by monkeys enable what epistemologists call Gettier cases to be recognised as cases of non‐knowledge.

744. Causa Sui
                    and Creationism in Spinoza
Ayalon, N.L. (2026). Causa Sui
                    and Creationism in Spinoza. Theoria.
https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70136
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This paper argues that Spinoza's definition of causa sui at the opening of the Ethics is a deliberate rejection of creationism. While most interpretations focus on whether causa sui expresses formal or efficient causation, they overlook the theological force of Spinoza's opening move. I suggest that the first definition—‘that whose essence involves existence’—mirrors and inverts Genesis 1:1, where Scripture begins with divine creation; Spinoza begins with self‐causation, replacing will and temporal beginning with necessity and eternity. Tracing the role of causa sui through Part I of the Ethics and culminating in the Appendix, I show that Spinoza systematically eliminates the conceptual basis of creation. If God is self‐caused, divine causation cannot be temporal or purposive. The critique of teleology and anthropomorphism follows directly from this shift. Reading causa sui against Genesis thus reveals a systematic connection between Spinoza's rejection of creationism and his critique of the theological foundations of morality, establishing the metaphysical conditions for the naturalistic ethics developed in the subsequent parts of the Ethics .

746. Justifying Parental Authority Without Ownership
Desyllas, J. (2026). Justifying Parental Authority Without Ownership. Journal of Libertarian Studies.
https://doi.org/10.35297/001c.173690
Abstract (original): Justifying parental authority is a problem for libertarian theory. Paternalism appears incompatible with self-ownership, yet parenting requires paternalistic control. This seems to present a dilemma: either deny the legitimacy of parental authority or deny that children are self-owners. This article first reconstructs the schools of thought that have grasped each horn. The equal-freedom school denies parental authority, and the homesteading school makes the parental role a property right. The article shows that both schools fail because of the same mistaken assumption that libertarianism denies enforceable parental obligations. It then extends the theory of causal parental responsibility from obligation to authority. Because parents voluntarily create a foreseeable state of peril, they incur a tort obligation to guide the child safely to independent adulthood. Limited parental authority is therefore not a property right but a derivative power, instrumentally necessary to discharge preexisting obligations owed to the child as a self-owner. This theory explains the origin, exclusivity, jointness, limits, and termination of parental authority and identifies it as an instance of a previously undistinguished category: derivative instrumental authority.

747. A Philosophy of Science Perspective on the Conscious Turing Machine
Wiese, W. (2026). A Philosophy of Science Perspective on the Conscious Turing Machine. Erkenntnis.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10670-026-01176-y
Abstract (original): Abstract The conscious Turing machine (CTM) is a model of consciousness developed by Lenore and Manuel Blum. Just as the Turing machine is an abstract formal model of computation, the CTM is an abstract formal model of consciousness. It provides a high-level description of processes that might be realised in conscious human beings and other conscious systems. Since it is not a model of brain function, it does not specify how it may be implemented in a living brain. The CTM might even entail false claims about the processes underlying consciousness in human beings. It is thus not obvious how the CTM can explain or contribute to our understanding of consciousness. This paper argues that the CTM may provide different forms of scientific understanding. It may provide a deepened understanding by generalising explanations provided by empirical theories of consciousness. It may also provide mathematical explanations of algorithmic properties. Whether the CTM actually provides these forms of understanding largely depends on whether it constitutes an accurate model of (human) consciousness. This is mostly an empirical question. Other forms of understanding, however, may not dependent on the CTM’s accuracy. Therefore, I also discuss to what extent the CTM may deepen our understanding of consciousness by enabling how-possibly explanations and non-explanatory understanding by unification.

748. Mnemic Extension and Appropriate Causation
Álvarez, J.F. (2026). Mnemic Extension and Appropriate Causation. Erkenntnis.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10670-026-01185-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Philosophers of memory often assume that the extended mind hypothesis is compatible with the causal theory of memory. For extracranial resources can, the idea goes, sustain the causal connection between a past event and a present representation of it that, on causalist views, distinguishes genuine remembering. Some go as far as to claim that constructive versions of the causal theory can accommodate complementarity-based versions of the extended mind hypothesis. This paper casts doubt on these ideas by defending two claims. First, the existing arguments for compatibility are flawed. While some conflate external information storage with cognitive extension, others wrongly hold that fulfilling the conditions for cognitive extension secures the appropriate kind of causal connection for remembering. Second, constructive versions of the causal theory and complementarity-based versions of the extended mind hypothesis are in fact incompatible. For constructive causal theories require of memory traces features that functionally disparate but complementary extracranial resources systematically lack. The paper concludes by considering how parity-based versions of the extended mind hypothesis might fare within the causal theory, and how other theories of memory might be assessed for compatibility with the extended mind hypothesis.

750. Governing Agentic AI: An Onto-Relational-Sophic (ORS) Framework for Deployment-Sensitive Governance
Ning, H. & Ding, J. (2026). Governing Agentic AI: An Onto-Relational-Sophic (ORS) Framework for Deployment-Sensitive Governance. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01546-w
Abstract (original): Abstract Agentic AI systems now act, rather than merely advise, within social and institutional settings. The persistent gap lies less in principles than in deciding which obligations a deployment should attract as the relationships around it change. Risk-based regulation classifies by declared intended purpose and does not register the dependence that forms around a system once it is in use. We propose the Onto-Relational-Sophic (ORS) framework, whose core contribution is to treat that dependence, rated as social embedding, as a trigger for reclassification. A system-in-deployment is rated on autonomy, social embedding, and moral relevance; each rating triggers a module of obligations on providers, deployers, and institutions; nine explicit rules govern assignment, conflicts, and reclassification, including the rating of multi-agent ensembles as wholes and the prospective rating of systems designed to induce dependence; and categories serve as summary labels. Two supporting pillars, grounded in Cyberism, structure the framework: a Cyber-Physical-Social-Thinking ontology that organises the evidence for each rating, and Cybersophy, an axiology that applies a reversibility priority when principles conflict. Three scenarios and a structured comparison with existing EU law show where ORS converges with current obligations and where it adds to them, most clearly when people come to depend on one certified system in different ways. No legal personality is conferred on any system. The rating scales and rules are proposed rather than validated: the social-embedding instrument has not been tested for inter-rater reliability, and the worked classifications rest on the authors’ own ratings. A reliability study should precede regulatory use.

Abstract (original): Abstract This article offers an ideology critique of the European Court of Human Rights’ jurisprudence on housing. 763. The Squirrel, the Symbol, and the Social: On Stephen Turner’s Naturalistic Reconstruction of Social Theory

Abstract (original): In Cognitive Science and the Reconceptualization of Social Theory , Stephen P. 765. Who Should Enforce Morality?
Radzik, L. (2026). Who Should Enforce Morality?. Crim Law Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11572-026-09816-8
Abstract (original): Abstract This essay evaluates Steven Wall's ethical environmentalist defense of legal moralism and legal paternalism. Section II summarizes Wall's account of the permissible use of state power to enforce broad morality and raises epistemic objections. Section III provides an argument for preferring alternative, social methods for enforcing ethics. Section IV presents some examples of duties of broad morality that are (with rare exceptions) left solely to social enforcement in the United States but that are backed by legal sanctions in many other countries–filial responsibility laws and bad Samaritan laws. These examples of legal moralism are built upon ethical judgments in which we have a lot of confidence, and so they lend support to Wall's position. However, in the end, I believe they reveal additional reasons for preferring social (rather than legal) methods for enforcing morality.

768. From law to practice: model-level operationalization of technical governance requirements for high-risk AI in healthcare
Cantú, E.D. et al. (2026). From law to practice: model-level operationalization of technical governance requirements for high-risk AI in healthcare. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01516-2
Abstract (original): Abstract The European Union published the Artificial Intelligence Act (AI Act) in a strategic effort to align the development and application of Artificial Intelligence (AI) in the common market. This act, one of the first laws of its kind, strengthened the development of trustworthy and human-centered AI through new regulations, prescribing requirements that providers of AI systems must conform to. The Act prescribes model-level technical requirements for high-risk AI systems, such as in terms of accuracy, robustness and fairness, which are to be addressed and reported in the form of conformity assessments. We identify, however, a significant gap between the high-level legal descriptions provided in the law and the low-level requirements needed for the implementation of automated assessment tools for algorithmic auditing. This gap is a major obstacle in the operationalization of AI Act compliance. The difficulties are aggravated due the lack of harmonized AI standards and the existing heterogeneity of AI models, datasets, and tasks. To address this gap, we present an open-source proof-of-concept assessment tool to operationalize the technical requirements of the AI Act according to the current regulatory landscape. With this tool, we demonstrate a bottom-up interpretation of the technical requirements of the AI Act for an automated evaluation of accuracy, robustness, and fairness. We apply our work to two neural network-based healthcare applications and discuss the requirements for further integration to initial conformity assessment and to extended application to post-market monitoring and other algorithmic auditing frameworks. With further work, the base structure presented here can be applied for integration beyond healthcare to other AI high-risk application domains.

770. Misconceptions of artificial intelligence: the modern seer as a synthesis of human knowledge without consciousness, emotion, or moral sill
Niazi, S.K. & Stańczak, M. (2026). Misconceptions of artificial intelligence: the modern seer as a synthesis of human knowledge without consciousness, emotion, or moral sill. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01527-z
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence is often depicted in public discourse as either a compliant statistical instrument or an emerging entity endowed with knowledge, creativity, feelings, and intentions that may soon rival human capabilities. Both portrayals are fundamentally flawed, as they rely solely on observable performance and infer internal experiences from social appearances. This Viewpoint advocates for a more rigorous model: conceptualizing artificial intelligence as a modern seer, an intricate system that compresses, retrieves, and recombines recorded human knowledge at scales unattainable by any individual. The Viewpoint develops the seer from a metaphor into an analytical framework. The seer is a consulted but uncertified epistemic authority: its capabilities generate interaction cues, the cues invite inferences of understanding, emotion, intention, and authority, and governance must act on these cues and on the calibration of trust rather than on claims about a machine mind. This model clarifies both the practical utility of these systems and the misguided authority often attributed to them. It demonstrates that synthesis can generate novel insights without consciousness, that affective language can provide reassurance without genuine concern, and that value-sensitive recommendations can be produced without moral agency. These distinctions do not render the technology inherently safe, as systems lacking experience can still amplify errors, entrench biases, concentrate power, and undertake consequential actions. The field of drug development exemplifies these limitations: a model may integrate evidence throughout the pharmaceutical process; nevertheless, biological validation and accountable judgment remain essential. This discussion is no longer solely philosophical; regulatory bodies such as the United States Food and Drug Administration, the European Medicines Agency, the International Committee of Medical Journal Editors, and prominent scientific publishers have each adopted frameworks to evaluate context, risk, credibility, and transparency, none of which directly address machine consciousness. Institutions have already moved beyond metaphor-based policymaking. This Viewpoint encourages scientists and scholars to do the same. It identifies ten misconceptions, eight category errors and two factual errors, traces each to the interaction cue that produces it, and states what the framework adds to existing debates on anthropomorphism, stochastic parrots, responsibility gaps, and context-based governance. Addressing these misconceptions will change how AI systems are tested, trusted, and governed.

773. Reasoning about risk and the Sure-thing Postulate
Kernohan, A. (2026). Reasoning about risk and the Sure-thing Postulate. Economics & Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0266267126100868
Abstract (original): Abstract Savage’s normative model of rational decision-making is deficient because the Sure-thing Postulate systematically ignores important information contained in lotteries. Useful statistical facts about lotteries, such as their modes and medians, are available as defeasible reasons for reasoned choices between lotteries. Because such summary statistics are holistic properties of lotteries, we can find cases where appealing to facts about modes and medians as normative reasons for choosing between lotteries conflicts with Savage’s Sure-thing Postulate. Such cases show the Sure-thing Postulate to be implausible and undermine both Savage’s argument for subjective expected utility theory and his argument for Bayesian subjective probabilism.

774. Will, preferences and the primacy of expert reports: navigating paternalism in Spain’s legal capacity reform (Law 8/2021)
Serra, M.L. (2026). Will, preferences and the primacy of expert reports: navigating paternalism in Spain’s legal capacity reform (Law 8/2021). International Journal of Law in Context.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s1744552326100718
Abstract (original): Abstract Spain’s Law 8/2021 marks a paradigm shift in disability rights, replacing substituted decision-making with a support-based model for legal capacity aligned with the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (CRPD). This article analyses the subsequent jurisprudence of the Spanish Supreme Court, investigating how it navigates the tension between individual autonomy and traditional paternalism. Drawing on theories of institutional conversion and cognitive dissonance, a qualitative analysis of seventeen binding judgments reveals a profound jurisprudential struggle. Findings highlight the primacy of medical-forensic reports over individuals’ expressed will, enabled by a weak interpretation of the legal duty to atender (to consider, rather than to follow). This institutional conversion undermines the reform, demonstrating how judicial practice perpetuates structural oppression and hinders the realisation of universal legal capacity.

777. Madness and Meaning: From Naturalistic Psychiatry to Narrative Transfiguration
Calderón, J. (2026). Madness and Meaning: From Naturalistic Psychiatry to Narrative Transfiguration. The Journal of Medicine and Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1093/jmp/jhag038
Abstract (original): Abstract Madness is the result of dysfunction of the brain, according to the standard story presented in biological psychiatry. Justin Garson calls this model of psychiatry madness-as-dysfunction. This way of conceiving psychiatric illness is not only reductive but it also renders those who suffer from maladies of the mind impotent before their dysfunctional brains. I call this model the bottom-up causal story, where dysfunction in genes causes dysfunction in brains which causes dysfunction in behavior. As a remedy for this way of thinking, Garson offers a new model, madness-as-strategy, where those who suffer can become participants in their own healing. In this model, there is a top-down causation in which the agent can initiate some effect on what ails them. However, in those persons suffering from psychotic experiences, top-down control is extremely limited. In this paper, I argue that we must also include a sense of horizontal causation in which others come alongside those suffering from psychosis. In fact, I argue that religious narratives can and do benefit to those suffering from psychiatric illness.

779. Sustainability in Charity Retail: Conflicts in (In)Sufficiency
Shaw, D. et al. (2026). Sustainability in Charity Retail: Conflicts in (In)Sufficiency. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06521-3
Abstract (original): Abstract Sufficiency has been receiving attention as a means to achieve sustainability. Insights on how to operationalise sufficiency in practice are limited and absent in the context of ecologically constrained organisations, such as, charities. Charities are viewed as critical to sustainability due to their reuse business model; however, charity income is dropping due to the saturation of low-quality and value clothing donations generated by ever-increasing production and consumption of (ultra)fast fashion. This is placing resource (e.g. reliance on volunteer labour) and ecological (e.g. finding reuse routes) burdens on an already constrained sector, highlighting the urgency for charities to future proof the retail businesses foundational to their fundraising efforts. To address this challenge, this research adopts an ethic of sufficiency to introduce business model innovation to charity retail. Drawing on interviews and observations with charity staff, collaborating experts and consumers as donor and retail users, we examine transition to a clothing rental model offering social and ecological sufficiency. We find that the two competing ethics of: (1) sufficiency representing enough and driving deceleration and (2) insufficiency representing not enough and driving acceleration, compete for primacy. While sufficiency can counter challenges arising from insufficiency, competition between them creates contradictory pressures for the charity. This is further exasperated by a misalignment between organisation and consumer experiences of sufficiency. The theoretical and practical implications of the findings for sufficiency, charity retail, rental and consumption are discussed.

780. From Integrity to Entangrity: A Diffractive Reading of a Sustainability Manager’s Entangled Subjectivity
Kalnitskaya, P. et al. (2026). From Integrity to Entangrity: A Diffractive Reading of a Sustainability Manager’s Entangled Subjectivity. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06535-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Integrity is one of the key concepts in business ethics and has conventionally been understood as a quality of the autonomous ethical subject. Relational approaches have challenged this view by showing how integrity is shaped through everyday human encounters and organisational practices. Feminist post-humanist thinking takes this challenge further by questioning human-centred understandings of ethical subjectivity itself. Drawing on Karen Barad’s articulations of entanglement and diffraction, this paper explores what happens to integrity when ethical subjectivity is understood as emerging through more-than-human entanglements. Through a collaborative diffractive reading of theoretical discussions on integrity and entanglement, alongside a sustainability manager’s empirical account, we trace three recurring patterns of entangled subjectivity: sensing that something is not right, carrying the weight of responsibility, and aspiring towards more liveable entanglements. These patterns show how integrity comes to matter through embodied, affective, material, and organisational entanglements. Building on these patterns, this research develops ‘entangrity’ as a relational and lively reconceptualisation of integrity, understood as an expression of entangled subjectivity. Entangrity renews questions surrounding ambiguous ethical subjectivities by pointing to the way ethical tensions are dwelt within, rather than ‘navigated’. It also exemplifies how diffractive reading can enable conceptual development whilst keeping different and even ontologically distinct theoretical perspectives in play. Thus, we invite thinking-with the possibilities of what ethical subjectivity and response-ability might become as posthuman business ethics continues to be developed.

781. Conversing without asserting: chatbots as interlocutors
Michel, J.G. & Green, M. (2026). Conversing without asserting: chatbots as interlocutors. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02627-6
Abstract (original): Abstract We argue that contemporary chatbots participate in conversations without making assertions, but instead by performing proto-assertoric acts (in a sense to be explicated) that are sensitive to the evolving needs of interlocutors, and in ways that help a conversation achieve its goal. In support of this claim we distinguish among different proto-assertoric stages including what we call ursertions and semisertions. These are utterances that can be evaluated for correctness while falling short of the epistemic and normative commitments characteristic of assertion. When appropriately sensitive to a shared conversational task, such utterances can nevertheless advance that task. We deploy this framework by treating conversations as goal-directed enterprises aimed at resolving practical or theoretical questions, within which such proto-assertoric interventions can play a substantive role. This account explains in a principled way how chatbots can participate in conversations without thereby attributing to them sensitivity to the distinctive normative commitments associated with assertion.

783. Instrumental convergence and power seeking
Thorstad, D. (2026). Instrumental convergence and power seeking. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02641-8
Abstract (original): Abstract Recent years have seen increasing concern that artificial intelligence may soon pose an existential risk to humanity. One leading ground for concern is that artificial agents may be power-seeking, aiming to acquire power and in the process disempowering humanity. I show how the argument from power-seeking rests on a strong version of a claim known as the instrumental convergence thesis. Abstract (original): Abstract The April 2025 integration of Ipsotek’s AI-driven video analytics software into Raptor Alert’s emergency management platform marks a significant escalation in the deployment of facial recognition technologies across U.S. public schools. Through a synthesis of Huey P. Newton’s (1972) theory of bureaucratic capitalism and the interpretive paradigm of Black Male Studies (Curry, 2017), this paper provides a clarifying framework for understanding the counterinsurgent logics at play within AI-augmented school surveillance regimes and their historically entrenched consequences for Black male students. 785. An explanationist analysis of causation
Kovacs, D.M. (2026). An explanationist analysis of causation. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02663-2
Abstract (original): Abstract The goal of this paper is to offer an analysis of causation in terms of explanation. The basis of the analysis is a plausible principle about “cause-tracing explanations”, i.e. explanations that cite causes: c causes e if and only if the fact that c causes e explains the fact that c explains e. I use this principle to develop an analysis of causation in terms of the role it plays in explanation. I then offer three arguments for my “explanationist” analysis: it scores well on the desideratum of parsimony; it answers the question of what distinguishes grounding from causation; and finally, it offers a compelling synthesis of two seemingly incompatible pictures of explanation and explanatory relations.

786. Escaping the understanding-assent links impasse
Walerius, J. (2026). Escaping the understanding-assent links impasse. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02666-z
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper provides an account of radical deviance, of what a speaker must do that would require us to translate her words non-homophonically. The account comes in two parts. The first argues that the appropriate unit of analysis for radical deviance is not a speaker’s utterance of individual sentences considered in isolation from her dealings with other speakers, but her contribution to a meaning-giving dialectic considered as a whole. The second argues that a speaker’s use of a word counts as radically deviant if only if it involves a refusal either (1) to engage in or (2) to respect the meaning-giving dialectic for that word. This account has two important consequences. First, it resolves a longstanding issue with semantic externalism, namely, that of explaining what a speaker must do to get thrown out of the social practice her participation in which secures for her words the meanings they have. Second, it provides a new contribution to the debate over the understanding-assent links thesis, according to which some sentences p are such that, necessarily, whoever understands p assents to p. Williamson (2007, 2012/2020) has argued that there are no such links. Boghossian (2012/2020) and Warren (2021) take themselves to have provided counterexamples to Williamson’s argument. Whether one considers these counterexamples to be genuine, however, is a matter of intuition. Thus the debate is at an impasse. The account of radical deviance proposed here provides a way out.

790. Coordinating Mental Imagery
Pani, S. (2026). Coordinating Mental Imagery. Analysis.
https://doi.org/10.1093/analys/anag092
Abstract (original): Abstract Mental imagery appears to be an essentially private phenomenon, and, for that reason, ill-suited to play a genuinely interpersonal role. This paper argues that intersubjective coordination of mental imagery is possible without sharing token imagistic representations. I distinguish two senses in which mental imagery might be shared and argue that neither is required for imagistic coordination. Imagistic cognition can be coordinated across agents without requiring shared mental images or fully identical contents, provided that their imagistic activities are governed by shared constraints and guided by common commitments. Drawing on work on shared perception and joint action, I contend that privacy at the level of mental tokens and phenomenal character does not preclude the interpersonal coordination of mental imagery in a functionally relevant sense.

Abstract (original): Scientists from the People’s Republic of China have become increasingly active in global climate change research. 795. Self-Sufficiency as a Political Virtue: Agency and Responsibility in the Age of Technology
Pelman, A. & Nachtomy, O. (2026). Self-Sufficiency as a Political Virtue: Agency and Responsibility in the Age of Technology. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10506-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Contemporary political philosophy has devoted extensive attention to technology, risk, and governance, yet it has paid little attention to the role of everyday material practices in shaping political virtue under industrial conditions. This paper argues that an increased degree of self-sufficiency, understood as domain-sensitive reduction of market reliance, should be recovered as a political virtue for technologically mediated societies. 796. Extended Virtues: From Human Character to Sociotechnical Moral Ecologies
Noller, J. (2026). Extended Virtues: From Human Character to Sociotechnical Moral Ecologies. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10507-1
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper argues that large language models (LLMs) and related technical systems are neither morally neutral instruments nor candidate bearers of virtue, but components of larger systems to which virtue terms can sometimes be applied. Drawing on virtue ethics, 4E cognition, and work on human–AI coevolution, it develops a three-level framework that distinguishes scaffolded , extended , and distributed virtue, and it specifies membership conditions under which a technical system becomes part of a virtue-bearing assemblage without thereby becoming virtuous itself. The framework is applied to an extended case study of justificatory practice in a regulatory agency, and three defeasible diagnostic indicators—removal, attribution, and correction—are proposed, to be applied jointly with those conditions in determining at which level a given sociotechnical arrangement operates. The paper further argues that alignment is better described as pseudo-habituation than as habituation: it reproduces the external profile of Aristotelian ethismos while lacking its constitutive features, and this disanalogy is precisely what blocks the ascription of virtue to models. The same coupling mechanisms that can stabilise epistemic and political virtues can entrench corresponding vices, and the paper closes with the governance implications of this ambivalence.

797. Can Political Choice Be Delegated to AI? Conscientiousness as Constitutive of Civic Virtue
Heyd, D. (2026). Can Political Choice Be Delegated to AI? Conscientiousness as Constitutive of Civic Virtue. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10509-z
Abstract (original): Abstract The deeper threat of AI technology to democracy does not consist merely in the abuse of the rules of fair elections, the use of fake material in the election campaign, or fraudulent election results, but in the subversion of the fundamental principles of a democratic regime: political participation, adequate representation and the very authority of the ensuing government. The thesis of the article is that there are no moral or political experts – human or artificial (algorithmic)—and that the delegation of the power to make political decisions to AI contradicts the basic liberal claim that the final arbiter of moral and political decisions is the individual human agent who autonomously and conscientiously takes personal responsibility. No sophisticated algorithm can truly represent our individuality and actual choice, since we are free to change our past attitudes and values which served as the basis for the algorithmic choice made on our behalf. Thus, even if we can in principle envisage a political community which is managed better by AI than by the existing human procedure, the value of both personal autonomy and of the traditional civic virtue of democratic engagement is superior to a computationally organized political order which would mean the loss of human agency. If human beings are “political animals”, then the cooperative enterprise of choosing a truly representative government, based on public Habermasian discourse, negotiations, grouping under parties and assuming responsibility cannot be replaced by a super-human – even an impartial, fair and efficient – algorithmic procedure.

801. Theology of the Algorithm

802. Contractualism and Two Types of Non-Identity
Clifton, O. (2026). Contractualism and Two Types of Non-Identity. J Moral Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1163/17455243-bja10195
Abstract (original): Abstract Choices that affect how well off future people will be often also affect who they will be. This generates “the Non-Identity Problem”: the problem of explaining why it would be wrong to create someone whose life would be worth living, rather than a numerically non-identical person whose life would be better. A growing number of contractualists say that their theory solves this problem, and to great effect. Contractualism is uniquely positioned to demonstrate that the choice at issue would be wrong because it would wrong someone, and thus to deliver an attractive, non-consequentialist theory of intergenerational ethics. I argue this is a mistake. Contractualism can neither solve the Non-Identity Problem nor deliver the theory of intergenerational ethics promised. This conclusion proves consequential not only for contractualist moral theory, but for intergenerational ethics, generally.
